#7210. Banking gets a helping hand in media modeling from SpendSight

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

It’s easy to know what your bank balance is, in the age of apps and online banking. It’s not as easy for banks to know how effective their marketing efforts are.

Mediastruction, an independent media agency that two years ago launched FutureSight, a SaaS platform dedicated to speeding up media mix modeling, is drilling into specific industries.

The brainchild of Marilois Snowman, who founded Mediastruction and FutureSight, SpendSight puts a specific lens onto the banking industry. It’s a free benchmarking tool that enables bank marketers to evaluate their media budgets to see if their spending aligns with their peers.

Snowman said conversations with clients and with other banks that aren’t yet clients told her there’s an information gap in the sector. “It’s a problem for mid market in general,” she explained in reference to other subscription media tools. “Sometimes mid-market brands don’t show up at all, or then they’re just wildly inaccurate.”

For small banks, this information is pretty important, agreed Mark Gibson, marketing practice leader at Capital Performance Group. “It’s not uncommon in the banking industry to have one or two people in the marketing department, and maybe not even an ad or media agency,” he said. “Media is like you know 1/20th of your job, so by definition, you’re not going to be very sophisticated about about what media is available and how you should allocate it. For that kind of advertiser, this kind of stuff can be really valuable.”

Snowman also noted that some media, from search to social and CTV, aren’t even properly tracked by other services. “Given the percentage of investment that is digital media these days — I mean, what isn’t digital media? — it just seems to be a big gap.”

Mediastruction built SpendSight from a proprietary 2026 survey of financial institution marketers, and Snowman said it’s the first of its kind to capture how banks and credit unions actually split paid media dollars across channels. To date, about 100 different banks and credit unions have participated in the survey, giving them access to the findings.

Any bank marketer enters their budget and channel mix, which lets SpendSight generate a side-by-side comparison against the peer average, flagging where they’re over- or under-invested. All inputs and responses to the survey remain anonymous and the underlying survey data is never exposed.

“All of that kind of discussion is really helpful for marketing teams to bring to executive leadership, and then to be able to sell through shifts in media plans and modeling engagements,” said Snowman.

So what has SpendSight determined? Initial results show some patterns with how banks and financial services have been marketing, and which media channels they’re relying on. Although it comes as no surprise that digital performance channels make up about 51% of the average media mix, broadcast and traditional media (linear TV, radio, print, out-of-home) still account for roughly 38%. (Interestingly, the American Bankers Association’s Banking Journal cited more than 60% digital spend, which Snowman attributed to differing channel definitions.)

Within digital, paid search and paid social represent more than one-fourth of average spend (13.7% and 13.0%, respectively). Paid search is the most widely used channel, with 96% of institutions investing in it. As for other media, direct mail makes up 7.4% of the average mix and is used by 82% of institutions, which is more than linear TV, display, and online video (as anyone knows who gets mail at home).

Gibson at Capital Performance Group, which participates in multiple surveys for banking including SpendSight, noted the reason he thinks SpendSight goes one step farther. “While [other studies] provide some useful benchmarking in terms of how much banks spend overall, and what various media are used, they don’t quantify the spend by media mix or by media channel,” he said. “That’s the new innovation that this tool does.”

It also gives Mediastruction lead generation to build up its potential client base with financial firms, which Snowman acknowledged. “What we really want to sell through is FutureSight’s modeling. We can use this as a free tool to get to know us to offer something of benefit to potential clients, that’s what our goal is.”

HUMAN Security’s latest investigation queries the effectiveness of CTV verification safeguards.

Advertisers continue to increase investments on the retail weekend but AI, tariffs and discounting pressure present banana skins.

#7206. In Graphic Detail: How shoppers are adopting AI for product research ahead of Black Friday

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Marketers are shifting ad dollars toward performance channels as the important Black Friday/Cyber Monday trading period approaches. Yet the way consumers use search — a crucial channel within performance marketing — is evolving rapidly, as they adopt generative AI tools like Gemini and ChatGPT to aid their purchasing decisions.

The places they find information about brands, the sources that information is based on, and what they do with that information are all changing. Meanwhile, audience adoption of AI research is far from uniform across generations and markets, further complicating the picture.

“Black Friday used to be won in the final week with the deepest discount and the loudest media,” said Jonathan Verrall, vp of SEO at Jellyfish. “Now AI writes the shortlist weeks earlier from the sources shoppers trust. Advertisers who treat Black Friday as pure promo media are already late. The winners will plan earned, owned and paid together from October and give AI data it can believe.”

What is certain is that the way brands seek to influence consumers during product discovery and research is shifting. With the year’s biggest sales event around the corner, read on for a data snapshot of the state of product discovery.

According to a survey of 2,800 shoppers in the U.S. and U.K. conducted by Brainlabs in 2025 and 2026, the percentage of consumers using AI for product research rose on average from 6% last year to 13%. Clearly, more shoppers are using AI tools to compare and research the products they’re considering. While search engines themselves still led consumer practices, sources like Amazon and YouTube slipped.

A Jellyfish study of citation sources, using U.K. Black Friday-related prompts the week beginning Nov. 16, found third-party sources leading the pack. That means blogs, news articles and comparison sites counted for more than brands’ own websites or retail listings in influencing AI chatbot representations of a brand or a product.

This is borne out by other studies. In an analysis run for an unnamed client, PMG found a brand’s website supplied just 24 out of 203 links cited in AI answers recommending the client’s products. Only 22 went directly to its homepage.

Even if they only account for a minority of citations, brands’ own sites, platforms and retailer listings are areas that an advertiser can control. Zooming in, not all retail platforms are equal, either. Amazon, Costco and Etsy’s sites have lost share of citations in recent quarters, while Target and Walmart have gained in recent months, according to data from Tinuiti and Profound.

The hope that they might influence shoppers using AI tools for product discovery has been one of the motivations driving marketing dollars toward ChatGPT. Ad spend has risen month-on-month, according to estimates by AdClarity, which estimated total spend on the platform to have reached $116.6 million in October, with U.S. brands accounting for 64%. (The above chart is in millions of dollars.)

Issues with measurability and evidence that ChatGPT ads lead to greater conversions, however, may hold brands back from accelerating that spend as much as OpenAI execs would like.

According to Bain, two-thirds of consumers use AI tools for general research, while 42% specifically use them to research products. But there are variations across generations.

The McKinsey Agentic Commerce Consumer Survey, June 2026, surveyed 5,800 shoppers who’s used AI chatbots or search tools for a shopping task in the last three months. It found middle-aged and younger consumers are embracing the tech while older shoppers (who still hold plenty of disposable income) aren’t. It’s an important distinction for brands targeting consumers in specific stages of their lives.

As social trends accelerate, brands like General Mills are scoring creator posts for shareability to bridge the gap between organic buzz and paid media.

Dick’s is betting that youth sports can fuel its next phase of ad growth through GameChanger—if it can overcome limits on scale.

As Black Friday Cyber Monday rapidly approaches, brands are most focused on acquiring new customers this holiday season.

#7204. The clipping economy is changing as platforms crack down and creators seek control

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Depending on who you ask in the creator economy, they might say that clipping is dead.

The practice of sharing short clips from long-form content to boost the original content’s audience or promote a brand, exploded in popularity over the last few years, with major creators like Jimmy “MrBeast” Donaldson, Cody “Clix” Conrod, and Eric “Airrack” Decker starting their own clipping companies. It catapulted certain creators into the spotlight and encouraged brands to hire clippers to give their branded partnerships extra airtime.

In short, what began as fans sharing clips has grown into a billion-dollar distribution business.

But some platforms are now tightening the rules around clipping. TikTok and YouTube have been cracking down on non-original and mass reposting content. Last week, Instagram added a content protection feature previously available only on Facebook, which lets eligible creators automatically identify videos matching their original content. They can now see how those videos perform and decide whether to hide them from people on the platform, add a credit link, or let the videos stay up. That gives creators more visibility and control over where their content appears, making clipping less of an open-ended reporting play and more subject to the original creator’s say.

“Creators deserve credit for the work they make,” a Meta spokesperson told Digiday. “[This] builds on our broader efforts to prioritize and reward original content and creativity over re-uploaded posts that don’t add a unique perspective or meaningful edits.”

And yet, platforms aren’t all moving in the same direction: Twitch is investing further into clipping to try and claw back the short-form real estate it’s ceded to other platforms. To understand what’s driving these diverging approaches, and what they mean in practice, Digiday spoke with seven experts across the clipping ecosystem.

“Ask 10 people [what clipping is] and you’ll probably get eight different answers,” said Darren Clover, CEO of creator-led holding company Full Box Inc., which owns Reach Clipping. “We define clipping as skilled editors taking existing source material and transforming it into unique pieces of short-form content…Those videos are then distributed across individual social accounts. That’s very different from downloading someone else’s TikTok and reposting the exact same video across hundreds of accounts.”

Opinions also differ around whether clipping works as a performance marketing channel or if it’s just a good distribution method. A few days ago, Brian Blum, host of the Only Sweat Equity podcast, posted on X that clipping “has actually always been dead.” He told Digiday he meant that it has never worked as a performance marketing channel.

Blum’s concern is that clipping can produce impressive view counts without reaching people likely to buy. He said campaigns can be vulnerable to fraud and attract audiences that may not be a fit for the product.

“It doesn’t have necessary protections to prevent viewbotting or fraud…So you’re actually just lighting money on fire,” he said.

Nikolai Amirzadeh, co-founded of clipping company Clip Tech, said clips can function as both marketing and distribution tools if the clipping company in question does the work to ensure views are valid.

“It is priced like performance, you pay per 1,000 verified views and nothing for clips that flop. It behaves like earned media, because it is native posts from real accounts, not ads. Brands typically pay $1 to $4 per thousand verified views, against $10 to $80 or more for traditional paid social,” Amirzadeh said. “Anything that looks off goes to the fraud team, and the clipper is not paid until it is cleared. A view is still a view by each platform’s own definition, which is a low bar. That is why the serious brands look at the top clips, watch time and what happens downstream, not just the view total.”

Then there’s the question of control: As clipping spreads and platforms tighten their rules, creators and their partners are increasingly concerned about how clips are edited and shared, and whether they could create brand-safety risks.

“Clipping is kind of a double-edged sword. It can help creators gain visibility and show that people value their content, but it could also get them flagged as spam,” said Ashray Urs, head of Streamlabs.

That lack of control gives creators and their managers pause, according to Reed Duchscher, CEO of Night Management. “It’s hard to control any of it,” he said. “Even when you put out a bounty for someone to clip something, you still have very limited control of what is posted, how it’s posted,” he added, pointing to the common practice of people clipping streamers out of context to farm outrage or drama.

Instead, their clients, like mega creator Kai Cenat, have their own in-house clippers, which allows for more control and, crucially, legitimate measurement.

Expect this to be an increasingly popular approach in the next year. “Sometimes with these clipping companies you can’t understand if the views are legitimate or not…you don’t know if they’re paid views or not paid views because you’re not controlling the TikTok accounts and not posting the videos. So we’re a little nervous about that,” Duchscher said.

With platforms cracking down on recycled content, the age of the clip farm may be over. But clipping itself isn’t.

Mustafa Aijaz, vp of media company SoaR, thinks clipping is no longer a low-effort gold rush. “A quick cut and post won’t get you very far,” he said. “You need a different caption, some commentary, or a breakdown — something that adds to the raw clip itself. If you’re different, the platform treats you differently. But if you look exactly like the other posts, you’ll likely be throttled.”

And as the demand for clips has grown, some have leaned into the more negative aspects of clipping, like taking creators out of context or clipping moments that are less than flattering. This influx of negative framing or drama farming has shifted creators’ opinions on external clippers.

Add that to the clipping companies’ increased selectiveness and rising rates, and something’s gotta give, according to Brandy Merriweather, founder and senior PR executive at BSTarPR.

“Many successful clipping pages have secured partnerships with gambling entities, music labels, creator management companies, creator collectives, and streaming platforms. Consequently, they are becoming more selective about which creators they post, their rates, and the frequency of posting for specific creators,” she said.

Merriweather told Digiday she’s seen creators have to pay extra money to clippers to switch priority streaming platforms.

“It’s a BIG business filled with egos, narrative control, fabricated numbers, unethical and sometimes purposely false content all going to the highest bidder,” she said. To avoid price gouging or unfair depictions, creators need to take clipping into their own hands. “What used to be a vehicle to gain visibility has now turned into an accountability stage that creators have no control over since many don’t own these pages. The creators, their families, and their teams are being used to create drama and fuel negativity which algorithms have prioritized because of the engagement….It’s a system many helped build that now controls them.”

Merriweather’s solution matches the other experts Digiday spoke with: creators have to create their own clipping networks to ensure they have control over their content and a proper understanding of how it’s performing.

Cody “Clix” Conrod is a professional Fortnite player with over 26 million followers and nine creator-focused businesses. Now he has his own holdco.

A look at how GTA 6 has impacted the creator economy, with stream numbers, viewership, top creators, and organic hype.

OpenAI revised its ChatGPT Business Hub terms following concerns that the original language could restrict businesses’ legal claims.

#7201. Digiday+ Research: A-Frame Brands, Ritual and other brands prioritize new customer acquisition this holiday season

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This research is based on unique data collected from our proprietary audience of publisher, agency, brand and tech insiders. It’s available to Digiday+ members. More from the series →

As Black Friday and Cyber Monday rapidly approaches, brands are most focused on acquiring new customers this holiday season. They’re also eager to turn those seasonal shoppers into repeat buyers.

Fifty-nine percent of brand and retailer professionals said that new customers are the primary audience for their 2026 holiday advertising. That’s down just one percentage point from the 60% of marketers who said the same last year, indicating that customer targeting trends have not shifted significantly.

Less than half of survey respondents (41%) said that existing customers are the main audience for their holiday marketing, up one percentage point from 40% in 2025.

The holiday season’s influx of shoppers gives brands an annual opportunity to attract new customers and to build lasting relationships beyond the initial purchase.

Ari Bloom, founder and CEO of brand incubator A-Frame Brands, estimates that brands see a 20% to 30% increase in new customers as a percentage of total during the holiday season, as consumers look to discover products and brands. “It is a time period where you can acquire a lot of new customers… when we naturally see new customers come into the holiday funnel,” Bloom said.

He also expects AI to play a central role in 2026 holiday marketing, changing how consumers find and buy products. “AI is probably going to become one of the most important funnels for new customers because, instead of using old habits to discover existing brands, there’s a whole other ‘person’ shopping for them,” Bloom said. “Just as we see people relying more on AI agents, you’re going to see more new brand exploration and customer acquisition from the brand side.”But acquiring new customers is only part of the equation. Brands also need to give existing customers a reason to return.

Laura Brodie, chief growth officer at supplement company Ritual, said brands can encourage repeat purchases by offering exclusive perks, seasonal products and other incentives. “Being able to pull people back who have shopped before by creating a compelling gift with purchase, seasonal drop or something similar is one way,” Brodie said. “If you don’t have the budget to support units, having something that feels intentional for an existing customer base is another.”

Brands with similar consumer bases should also consider partnering on gift-with-purchase offers, according to Brodie. “The cost of acquisition is high during the holidays, so being able to tap an existing audience can be really cost-effective,” she said. “Similar-sized brands can create something that feels compelling [to consumers] and benefits both brands — potentially an offer swap. We’ve had success partnering with brands like Osea on a swap gift with purchases.”

As social trends accelerate, brands like General Mills are scoring creator posts for shareability to bridge the gap between organic buzz and paid media.

Dick’s is betting that youth sports can fuel its next phase of ad growth through GameChanger—if it can overcome limits on scale.

Product discovery habits are changing, leaving marketers unsure how to pivot ahead of the biggest sales period of the year.

#7200. Product Manager - Postgres at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We're hiring a Product Manager to own Postgres itself at Supabase. Every Supabase project is a full Postgres database, and Postgres is the foundation that Auth, Storage, Realtime and Edge Functions run on. You'll own the product experience of the database: Postgres versions and upgrades, maintenance, observability, extensions and security.

You'll work closely with our Postgres engineering teams and with the PM lead for Databases, who leads Multigres. You'll define the Postgres requirements that architecture must meet, and own how customers experience their database through that transition.

This role is ideal for someone who loves Postgres, sweats the details that make a database trustworthy, and is excited about improving the experience for millions of developers. Small improvements here compound across every project on the platform.

You'll own Postgres versions, upgrades and end-of-life. Which major and minor versions we support, how and when customers upgrade, and how we retire old versions. Your goal is to make upgrades routine rather than risky.

You'll make maintenance predictable. You'll design how we schedule, communicate and run maintenance windows so customers stay informed and in control, and disruption is rare and expected.

You'll help developers understand their database. Query performance, metrics, logs and advisors: you'll own the path from "my database is slow" to the fix.

You'll own our extension ecosystem. Which extensions we offer, how we evaluate and support new ones, and how we keep them working across versions.

You'll own Postgres security. Roles and permissions, Row Level Security ergonomics, secure defaults, and how we respond to Postgres and extension CVEs.

You'll talk to developers across the full spectrum, from indie developers on their first project to enterprises running critical workloads, and bring what you learn into every prioritization call.

You'll own the problem definition, requirements and success metrics for what we ship, such as upgrade success rate, the share of the fleet on supported versions, and time-to-diagnose for performance problems. You'll set the metric before the team builds, then track it after launch.

You'll keep Engineering, Support and GTM aligned. You'll own the plan of record for what's coming, why, and in what order, and communicate version changes and deprecations clearly to customers.

4+ years of Product Management experience on databases, developer tools or infrastructure, or equivalent depth as an engineer, DBA or founder moving into product. You're comfortable commenting on an architecture doc and advocating for the user when trade-offs are required.

Know Postgres well, or another relational database well enough to ramp quickly. You're comfortable reading EXPLAIN output and talking about replication, WAL, roles and extensions.

Have shipped products where reliability and trust are the experience. You treat downtime and breaking changes as product problems, not just operational ones.

Write clearly. Your specs, announcements and deprecation notices leave little room for confusion.

Build with what you own. You run queries, test upgrades and try extensions yourself, and form opinions through first-hand use.

Use AI to move faster. You use AI tools to compress research, drafting and feedback synthesis so you spend more time on judgment calls.

Work async by default. Supabase is fully remote. You make decisions in writing and move work forward without meetings.

Ran Postgres in production: upgrades, tuning, incident response.

Worked on database observability, performance tooling, or security and compliance products.

Contributed to Postgres, its extensions, or the wider open-source community.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

#7196. In Graphic Detail: The GTA 6 creator industrial complex

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

The most anticipated modern video game, Grand Theft Auto 6 (GTA 6), was released a week ago today, selling $3.79 billion in just a single day of sales. Creators have been preparing for GTA 6’s release for years, figuring out ways to stand out from the millions playing the game and the thousands of people making content around it.

According to analytics website Stream Charts, GTA 6 beat every other major game’s full-week watch time in just two days, and did so with ease, hitting 214.6 million hours watched in two days, versus the next highest game, Valorant’s 150.48 million hours in a week.

“A record was never in doubt. Rockstar’s gameplay reveal in August already drew close to 4 million concurrent viewers, for a game nobody could play yet,” Streams Charts product manager Nazar Babenko told Digiday.

A week later, let’s take a look at how the GTA 6 creator economy over the last week (and the months leading up to it), including the livestream watch hours, the most popular platforms, the top streamers, and the organic cultural conversation around the game. Just how big is GTA 6? Unsurprisingly: Huge.

Ahead of GTA 6’s launch, platforms saw a marked increase in content around the franchise and the previous game, GTA 5, which launched in 2013. Streamlabs reports that GTA 5’s year-to-date viewership was higher than several other major, more current video games.

Streams Chart’s seven-day GTA 6 numbers showcase the game generated more watch time on Twitch alone than any previous game launch had across all platforms the company tracks.

“Hours watched surprised us more than the peak,” said Babenko.

Creators who built a name and audience for themselves making GTA 5 content have been preparing for GTA 6 for over a year (the game release, initially slated for November 2025, was delayed twice, and the game was officially announced in 2023).

Reed Duchscher, CEO and founder of talent agency Night Media, said that GTA creator Andre “Typical Gamer” Rebelo (who has 16.3 million followers on YouTube), has perfected his video-on-demand content (VOD) with GTA 5 and was prepared for GTA 6. “He’ll have a bunch of video concepts that he’ll go film away from his livestream and then post on YouTube because most people will be so concerned about beating the campaign,” Duchscher said. “He has a wealth of knowledge from GTA 5 and a huge catalog on YouTube, so we know what video concepts are going to do well.”

According to Streams Charts, there were over 125 million hours watched of GTA 6 gameplay in just 24 hours (November 18 to November 19). Twitch drove the most views, accounting for 1.7% of views (market intelligence firm Sensor Tower reports Twitch mobile app downloads and daily active users increased by more than 80% day over day on those two days), then YouTube, which pulled in 35.1%. Kick came in third with 22.1%, while other live streaming platforms made up the difference – which was still over 1.3 million hours of watch time.

Notably, YouTube organized a 24-hour livestream with some of its top creators, but was still beaten by Twitch, possibly because the platform announced during TwitchCon 2026 that it would be giving away copies of the game through surprise daily drops. Both Twitch and YouTube added custom GTA 6 badges, animations, and effects.

Though Twitch edged out YouTube for livestream watch hours in the first day of GTA 6’s launch, YouTube still saw over 3.5 billion global views of videos with GTA 6 in the title the day of release. To date, YouTube reports the Grand Theft Auto series has over 900 billion views, and that GTA 6 videos hit 40 billion views before November 18.

Within the first week since launch, there have been 420 million hours watched of GTA 6 across all major platforms, with peak viewers hitting 7.91 million. Stream Charts reports the game has been mentioned in livestream chats over 3.2 million times across more than 55,000 channels, with sentiment almost split between positive and negative – that could be largely attributed to the online reactionary response to some gameplay elements that involve fixing up a nice and being nice to your girlfriend.

Unsurprisingly, perhaps the biggest streamer in the world, Darren Jason “IShowSpeed” Watkins Jr., drew the most eyes during the first 24 hours post-release, with 4.7 million watch hours across YouTube and Twitch. Close behind him was a streamer known as The Burnt Peanut, with 3.8 million watch hours across YouTube, Twitch and Kick.

In third was Brazilian streamer Alan “Alanzoka” Ferreira, who notably generated 3.1 million hours watched during launch day solely on Twitch.

In just a week, GTA 6 has edged out some of the most popular streaming categories and video games, drawing more views than Kick’s entire IRL stream category, and drawing close to its Just Chatting category. As of halfway through 2026, Just Chatting had become the most-watched category across all major streaming platforms, signaling that they were shifting from gaming-focused content to more IRL and commentary content. But GTA 6 appears to have shifted the livestream platforms back to what they were first made for: Gaming.

A broad “gaming” category is the most-watched on all of YouTube within the last month, and the fourth-most watched on Twitch. Even GTA 5, the previous installment in the franchise, ranked in the top ten most-watched categories over the last 30 days. Gaming is back, and creators will likely lean further into its popularity, especially those who don’t typical game.

According to Streams Charts, 1,056 channels reached a new all-time peak viewership record while streaming GTA 6, with the most records held by YouTube creators. According to the week after launch report, almost one in five record-breaking channels received at least ten times as many concurrent viewers as their pre-GTA 6 peak.

According to data pulled by influencer marketing agency Linqia, the GTA 6 hype was almost entirely organic. Its creator data pulled during the pre-launch GTA 6 period (April 1 through November 18), shows that 97.8 percent of creators posting about GTA 6 were doing so organically, with just 2.2 percent sponsored. Rockstar Games did not pay a single creator who took part in Linqia’s study, while 7 percent of the paid creators came from prediction market Kalshi – notably not a gaming brand.

“The most active brands were the platforms,” said Babenko. Two-thirds of creators talking about GTA 6 never even mentioned a brand. According to Linqia, it seems sponsorships might actually hurt content performance: sponsored posts that name-dropped GTA 6 performed 3 percent worse than each creator’s typical sponsored post. You can’t fake the GTA 6 hype.

“I think a lot of brands leaned in from an organic social standpoint but many outside of gaming didn’t think creator first for GTA,” said Keith Bendes, chief strategy officer at Liniqia. “Which was a big missed opportunity since creators were clearly talking organically about the game in droves.”

Cody “Clix” Conrod is a professional Fortnite player with over 26 million followers and nine creator-focused businesses. Now he has his own holdco.

The creator economy is shifting its stance on clipping as platforms crackdown; what does clipping look like in the future?

OpenAI revised its ChatGPT Business Hub terms following concerns that the original language could restrict businesses’ legal claims.

We are looking for a Product Builder to join the IDP & RPA pod. You will take real problems from customers, decide what is worth solving, and build and ship the solution yourself, using AI tools to get from idea to production quickly without lowering the bar.

Product Building is how Camunda's Experience organization builds the applications people use on top of Camunda 8, such as Hub, Operate, and ProcessOS. Small pods of builders own a product area from customer problem to shipped change, with no hand-offs between product management, design, and engineering. We are setting this model up now, so you will help shape how it works.

The IDP & RPA pod builds how Camunda processes work with documents and legacy applications. With intelligent document processing (IDP), customers create extraction and classification templates in Camunda Hub and publish them so a process can use AI to turn invoices and other documents into structured data. The hard problems come with putting LLMs into customer processes: extraction quality, model and prompt choices, testing and trust, and working for AI agents as well as for people.

Help shape product direction for IDP & RPA by combining customer input, usage data, and field feedback into clear priorities.

Partner with the other builders and product strategy to turn problems into shipped features, using short written proposals and prototypes to align early.

Make judgment calls with incomplete information within your scope and own the outcome.

Communicate trade-offs and decisions clearly to stakeholders who don't live in the details the way you do.

Use AI tools across the whole loop, from specs to prototypes to production-ready code, and hold a high bar for what you ship by reviewing and improving the output rather than accepting it.

Build, test, and ship improvements to the IDP & RPA experience, reading errors and tracing issues far enough to give your pod useful context.

Talk to customers and to support and customer success colleagues about how IDP is used and where it breaks, and turn what you learn into decisions about what to build next.

Pick up customer incidents and trace them across Hub, the connectors, and the deployed process of the customer.

Track what you ship against real outcomes and adjust when the data tells you to.

#7193. Dick’s Sporting Goods is turning youth sports into a premium ad business

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Dick’s Sporting Goods has found that there’s big business in youth sports.

The sporting retailer recorded a third quarter GAAP net income of $161 million in earnings this week — more than double the $75 million reported the year prior.

Dick’s execs attributed much of that success to Dick’s Media Network, home to its youth sports app GameChange.

Like many long-tail retail media networks, Dick’s Sporting Goods doesn’t break out its ad business revenue by dollar figure or percentage. Dick’s chief financial officer Navdeep Gupta noted that the company is grappling with supply-chain related costs, higher fuel costs and other pressures, but Dick’s Media Network partially offset those pressures.

“We have talked about these gross margin growth drivers with Dick’s Media Network and GameChanger. Those are continuing to be very differentiated drivers of not only our profitability, but our gross margin expansion,” he said.

Dick’s Media Network is four years old and offers ad units in-store, on its own site, off-site across the open web, social and streaming. Perhaps its biggest get is its live sports via GameChanger.

“They found this gap in the marketplace in terms of sports club video streaming. And they’ve created this app, this solution, and now they inherently have their own O&O [owned and operated] inventory from a media perspective,” said Olivia Hawkins, vp of media strategy at Salt XC.

Dick’s has previously worked with sports brands like Adidas, On, Under Armor and Crocs. However, the sports retailer is actively engaging non-endemic partners too.

Take Chobani, for example. The yogurt brand bought ad space on Dick’s Sporting Goods Game Changer app during the 2026 FIFA World Cup, said Hawkins, who was familiar with the deal.

She did not disclose financial details of the ad buy. The campaign focused on reach and awareness, getting Chobani in front of youth sports enthusiasts, like parents and coaches, Hawkins said.

“They met our expectations in terms of reaching a new audience and delivery, frequency, and all those media metrics — CPM and video completions and all that,” she said, without detailing the campaign’s success.

Dick’s video app CPMs are more premium, hovering around $35, according to one commerce executive who spoke on the condition of anonymity.

“That’s more expensive than big TV. They’re asking for that price for that type of inventory. It’s a great solution — super clever, but it’s expensive,” the anonymous exec told Digiday. In comparison, Netflix CPMs fell from their initial $60 when it launched in 2022 to the low $20s currently.

Seemingly, brands are willing to cough up the dough. That’s the bet Dick’s seems to be making. It makes sense given sports has become the industry’s latest golden goose.

Put some numbers on it. In a survey this year, Dick’s Media and eMarketer reported that 63% of U.S. parents have kids in youth sports and 84% of them said youth sports influence their buying choices.

Now, there’s the question of scale. Dick’s GameChanger app has more than 5 million monthly active users, but youth sports is more niche than something like the National Football League, which averages 18.7 million viewers per game during the regular season.

“It’s a great audience. It’s a great inventory, but it is finite versus other offsite environments that include all of the open web,” Hawkins said.

That leaves Dick’s in the same boat as other retail media networks — or at least every other RMN that isn’t Amazon or Walmart — where continued growth may have to come from beyond retailers’ owned and operated channels.

As social trends accelerate, brands like General Mills are scoring creator posts for shareability to bridge the gap between organic buzz and paid media.

As Black Friday Cyber Monday rapidly approaches, brands are most focused on acquiring new customers this holiday season.

Product discovery habits are changing, leaving marketers unsure how to pivot ahead of the biggest sales period of the year.

#7189. Brands race to systematize how to know what makes creator content worth paying for

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Social media trends move faster than brands can keep up with. Now, they’re trying to create repeatable systems to turn organic social attention into something they can evaluate, fund and ultimately scale.

Take General Mills, for example. Home to brands like Cheerios, Cinnamon Toast Crunch and Trix, the CPG brand has spent the past year putting together an internal framework to systematize monetizing viral moments.

Its “talkability score” is a calculation used to measure whether General Mills’ brands are earning more attention with social media creators than they’re buying with paid ads. It’s a loose framework that varies by brand, Mindy Murray, director of influencer and content at General Mills said, and generates a score based on whether users shared it, put their own spin on it and how long interest in the trend lasts.

Murray was mum on the actual mechanics on how it actually determines whether to invest ad spend. “We’re weighing on earning, we’re weighing things like active engagement — so shares likes, comments, saves — because we know that that means someone took an actual effort to do something versus just an impression,” Murray said.

Virality can’t be manufactured, but this “operating system” allows them to “go faster and be smarter,” said Murray.

Over the past year, General Mills has also reorganized its teams to bring social and influencer marketing under Murray’s purview, she told Digiday, to move more quickly and efficiently in converting buzzy organic content into paid ads.

For example, over the summer Reese’s “Eat ‘Em Up” cereal jingle from the early 2000s started trending on TikTok. General Mills noticed the trend, partnered with GloRilla to re-record the rap, tracked real-time activity via its framework, and ultimately turned it into a campaign on streaming platforms and Reese’s Puffs’ YouTube channel. The company boasted a 2.5% year-over-year sales increase as a result, but did not provide specific sales figures. The company did not say specifically how the framework informed that strategy.

Last week, influencer agency Billion Dollar Boy (BDB) rolled out its Creator Investment Planner, a financial modeling tool that aims to help marketers understand the ad spend levels required to plan and “de-risk creator investments,” according to a news release.

BDB’s tool works by calculating blended CPMs from creators and social media platforms. Marketers enter their budget and time frame, and the platform calculates estimates for share of voice, total impressions and earned media value. Per BDB, the model can update as inputs change, where marketers can expect to “de-risk” spend commitments.

And in April, CreatorIQ creator platform and creative analytics and governance platform CreativeX launched a new tool to help brands incorporate creator content into a paid media strategy. The system works on an API, sending creator content from CreatorIQ’s platform to CreativeX to be graded, reviewed by CreativeX’s AI-powered system and optimized by a brand campaign manager. Ultimately, it aims to identify creator posts with potential to do well as an ad before any media buyers open their wallets. Nestlé was the first brand to sign on. The integration was announced on an invite-only basis.

To agency execs, all signs point to brands getting more rigorous about their spend — especially when it comes to squeezing performance out of creators — and do it before the trend disappears.

Creator spend is expected to increase 12% every year until 2030, according to advertising research firm Madison & Wall. In no small part, brand spend on boosting or promoting creator content is fueling that growth.

“Before any sort of budget is unlocked, you have to be able to prove out that there’s a higher propensity for that piece of content for whatever investment you’re making into social to actually move towards a conversion,” said Madison Gaudry-Routledge, evp of social at Viral Nation, a social agency.

Most brands, Gaudry-Routledge said, haven’t come up with a named framework in the same way General Mills has. Brand spend on creator marketing is expected to grow twice as fast as spending on advertising. Naturally, brands are eager to see proof of concept as early as possible.

Viral Nation has its own proprietary framework offered to clients as an add-on service. It’s called Culture Quotient that blends brand awareness, brand affinity and brand velocity into a measurable score to determine a brand’s relevance in cultural conversation. The tool works via AI that analyzes conversations and content, identifies emerging cultural trends and tracks shifts in consumer sentiment, per Gaudry-Routledge. The social exec declined to share any client-specific examples.

These calculations are being built in real time and haven’t yet replaced traditional metrics like share of voice, audience sentiment and engagement.

“As an industry, we’re continuing to try to catch up on operationalizing frameworks and systematizing measurement and still not there yet,” said Kristina Coughlin, svp and general manager of Trevant, a creator marketing agency.

Dick’s is betting that youth sports can fuel its next phase of ad growth through GameChanger—if it can overcome limits on scale.

As Black Friday Cyber Monday rapidly approaches, brands are most focused on acquiring new customers this holiday season.

Product discovery habits are changing, leaving marketers unsure how to pivot ahead of the biggest sales period of the year.

#7186. The gaming creator with more than 26 million followers just launched his own holding company

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is part of an ongoing series examining successful creator business strategies. More from the series →

Cody “Clix” Conrod is a professional Fortnite player who regularly wins esports tournaments, and is also a wildly popular creator. Conrod started playing video games at five years old, and began live streaming at 13. Conrod has amassed some 26 million followers across social media since (8.73 million followers on Twitch, over 5 million on YouTube, 6.7 million on TikTok, 2.86 million on X, 2.81 million on Instagram, and 848,000 on Snapchat).

He’s the most-watched professional Fortnite player in the world, according to his team, but in the last few years, he’s been looking to grow beyond just esports content and branded partnerships with a portfolio of his own companies.

While still with talent agency Vayner Sports, Conrod was paired with agent Darren Glover, who said he was influenced by the career movements made by creators like Jimmy “MrBeast” Donaldson and the Dude Perfect team (who were forming their own entities and corporations). Glover wanted to apply this same approach to Conrod’s career — prioritizing fewer, more quality partnerships.

“I tried to actually do half and half. I tried to be a halftime Fortnite professional and then a halftime content creator that also wants to work with brands. And I became not good at the game,” Conrod said, which pushed him to retain Glover.

Glover was named CEO of Full Box Inc., the Conrod-led holding company that launched in early October, with Conrod as chief creative officer. Heading into this fall, Conrod was running his entire creator business through Clix Gaming, his creator-focused gaming media company. Full Box Inc. combines Conrod’s nine creator-focused companies and organizes them under three pillars: operating companies (Clix Gaming, Clix Creative, and Dr3amin Clothing), Full Box Ventures (Reach Clipping, Real Sports App, water filtration company Rorra, TapCaps PC collectibles, and lifestyle brand XSET), and Impact (Dr3amin Access Fund).

“The change was not starting something new, it was adding an umbrella over [the] top of everything,” said Glover.

Conrod may be following in the footsteps of other massive creators with millions of followers, but his positioning as a professional gamer first, content creator second, means his approach has to consider both professions. Sometimes, Conrod can’t stream his Fortnite gameplay during tournaments, as creators playing online games can often be “stream sniped,” which is when viewers watch a broadcast while also playing the same game and use the stream to find and kill the streamer in-game. This can cause players who are stream-sniped to lose tournaments. Glover helped Conrod scale his content to a point that, if he does go to a Fortnite tournament and isn’t streaming, he has a backlog of things to post that doesn’t require him to work late into the night to produce something post-tournament.

The holdco is the biggest step of many Conrod has taken to ensure his career can extend far beyond professional gaming. It’s one many video game creators may take in the future as they seek to generate revenue that isn’t tied to game updates or platform algorithms — or even their own face. Consider it the MrBeast business model.

“I realized when I was 18, I wanted to build stuff larger than just me, where it could scale without my face all the time or me having to be there,” said Conrod.

Conrod and Glover don’t just want to create products and agencies and sign brand deals, but extend into the philanthropy side of the creator space to cement his legacy.

Conrod launched the Dr3amin Access Fund, which provides kids with the access and hardware needed to become professional gamers or gaming streamers. Last year, the fund delivered more than $250,000 in gaming equipment.

For now, partnerships still make up around half of Conrod’s revenue, with the balance split between ad revenue on Twitch, YouTube, owned royalties, and Fortnite’s unique Support-A-Creator program, which lets players back creators in Fortnite by giving them 5 percent of purchases made with their unique creator code. They did not share revenue specifics.

“Our business is profitable and runs without one single brand dollar,” said Glover. “So brand dollars for us are investment vehicles of partnerships for us to do more, but we’re not putting ourselves at risk of being profitable or scaling our company based on other people’s dollars.”

He wants more creators to understand that they have some control over their revenue on platforms like YouTube and Twitch, or, in Conrod’s case, Fortnite, and that they can expand beyond just a basic sponsorship setup.

“I want content people to feel like they are a major factor for the health of the business,” Glover added, “and I think we’ve been showing that we’re doing that, and we’re doing it really well.”

A look at how GTA 6 has impacted the creator economy, with stream numbers, viewership, top creators, and organic hype.

The creator economy is shifting its stance on clipping as platforms crackdown; what does clipping look like in the future?

You'll run a quarterly win/loss program and share what you learn openly, build a clear path for sales leads on our website, and make sure launches that matter to larger accounts ship with both a self-serve and a sales-assisted version. Your work has a direct impact on helping Kit reach $100,000,000 ARR while protecting the self-serve engine that got us here.

Segmentation and buyer definition: turning firmographic and product usage data into a clear picture of who to sell to and when

Messaging and positioning for a sales-assisted buyer, including competitive differentiation and objection handling

Sales enablement that reps actually use: demo narratives, discovery guides, business cases, and battlecards

Writing and storytelling that turns product depth into a business case a non-technical buyer can act on

Working with CRM, product analytics, and pipeline data to measure funnel conversion, win rate, and deal size

Customer and market/competitor research, including win/loss interviews and call analysis

Systems thinking: connecting people, processes, data, and tools to identify opportunities and build repeatable solutions across the go-to-market motion

Strong AI fluency: knowing when to leverage AI and how to build AI-powered workflows or tools that scale product marketing work, with human judgment and quality checks built in

Pricing and packaging input for a new tier or add-on (nice to have)

7+ years in B2B SaaS product marketing, including at least 2 years supporting a sales team selling to mid-market and/or larger accounts

Added a sales-led motion on top of a product-led one at a high-growth company, and can show what changed before and after with clear metrics

Built a deal toolkit and enablement program from little or nothing, and tied it to a sales metric like win rate or deal size

Ran a win/loss or customer research program that changed how a team sold or built

Ran an expansion or account-based program for an existing customer base (nice to have)

Worked in email, martech, or the creator economy

You lead without a formal title. You align sales, product, RevOps, and finance on goals, trade-offs, and what success looks like, then keep things moving.

You advocate for creators in decisions, and you protect the self-serve experience instead of pushing every account toward sales.

You act with urgency and stay focused on hitting OKRs and owning outcomes.

You work in public. You share findings, drafts, and the reasoning behind decisions so others can build on them.

You connect data to decisions, and you help others get sharper about using it.

Experience in only self-serve or only enterprise sales. This role lives in the space between them, and you'll need to understand both.

Wanting a sales playbook already in place. Much of this motion doesn't exist yet, and you'll be building it as you learn.

Preferring to be measured on output rather than outcomes. You'll share accountability with Sales for win rate, deal size, and expansion revenue.

Needing extensive approval before acting. You'll be trusted to make calls on speed versus quality and to explain your reasoning.

Discomfort with direct, candid feedback, especially from sellers on what's working in live deals.

We work mostly on East Coast time—so if you're based on the West Coast of the United States, Europe, or Asia, you might have a few early mornings or late evenings. #7173. Attorney, New Verticals at Atticus

At any given time, 16 million Americans are experiencing a crisis that requires urgent help from our legal system or government. The right assistance could transform their lives. But today, most never get it.

Atticus makes it easy for any sick or injured person in crisis to get the life-changing aid they deserve. In the last six years, we've become the leading platform connecting people with disabilities to government benefits. We’re also quickly scaling an injury practice to help victims of accidents, misconduct, and violence get compensation from insurance.

So far, we've helped hundreds of thousands of people access over $10B in life-changing aid — and earned over 20,000+ five-star reviews. And we're just getting started.

We've raised more than $100 million from top VC firms like Forerunner, GV (Google Ventures), and True Ventures, with ambitions to create a category defining business assisting Americans in need.

Each month, tens of thousands of Americans seek our help for a single legal matter – applying for Social Security Disability, filing a Workers’ Compensation claim – but many of these clients are actually eligible for additional aid without ever knowing it.

Your job as Lead Attorney, New Verticals is to help us do right by these clients, helping them claim additional benefits through the social safety net. You’ll oversee the team of intake specialists who speak with clients who have secondary claims, evaluating the case facts and whether to connect them with an Atticus attorney or local attorney to provide legal advice and potentially take on their case.

This is a job that straddles the line between practicing the law and growing a business. It requires developing a strong understanding of multiple fields of law, and being able to accurately identify when we can help and when we cannot. But even more importantly, this job requires you to be an incredibly strong operator: you need to keep the big picture in mind and stay laser-focused on how we can achieve our goals, oftentimes finding outside-the-box ways to accomplish them. You will report to our General Manager of New Verticals and work closely with our CEO and Managing Attorney.

On any given day, you’ll spend your time:

Speaking with severely injured clients trying to rebuild their lives after a catastrophic accident

Sourcing and vetting local counsel in individual markets, holding a high bar for representation quality and ending relationships that fall below it

Working with our data and insights team to identify existing clients who may have unrecognized claims in adjacent fields of law, and setting the standard for which ancillary cases we take on

Building the playbook for claim types we haven't handled before, starting by screening cases yourself and eventually creating the protocol our intake specialists will run at scale

This is a rare opportunity for an attorney to join a profitable and growing public-interest law firm and take on one of our biggest priorities as a company.

You can understand the nuances of various contingency fields of law and can apply them to a variety of cases (including Social Security Disability, Workers’ Compensation, Personal Injury, and more) – you don’t need to be an expert today, but you should be a fast learner.

You can empathize with anyone and quickly build rapport and relationships while exercising good judgment, both with our clients and local counsel at law firms across the country.

You have strong instincts and judgment as an operator – you can run effective and efficient processes, and you don’t let them run you.

You have a demonstrated commitment to doing good and helping people.

You’ve built a track record as a great people manager – you know how to motivate talent and hold a high bar for output.

You have meaningful work experience outside of serving as a lawyer, ideally at a startup or other fast-paced, agile environment (e.g., a business operations team).

We are strongly committed to building a diverse team. If you're from a background that's underrepresented in tech, we'd love to meet you.

This role offers a unique chance to join a startup that has strong traction (substantial funding, well-respected backers, tremendous growth, and many happy customers) but is still small enough that you can have a huge impact and play a role in shaping our success.

We're a certified B Corporation tackling a critical social problem. Our mission to help people in need drives everything we do, and your work here will touch many lives.

We offer competitive pay — including equity — and generous benefits:

Medical and dental insurance with 100% of employees and their families premiums covered

15 vacation days & ~20 paid holidays each year (including two weeks at end-of-year)

$1,000 reimbursable stipend for education and training outside of work

Up to $1,200/year student loan repayment assistance

Humble, thoughtful, smart, fun colleagues

We anticipate the base salary band for this role will be between $140,000 and $175,000 in addition to equity and benefits. The salary at offer will be determined by a number of factors such as candidate's experience, knowledge, skills and abilities, as well as internal equity among our team.

This job is fully remote and we’re committed to empowering everyone with flexibility. Work remotely, and travel to LA (on the company dime) as needed to be with your colleagues – usually quarterly, plus offsites. We care a lot about building a great culture and we think some interactions need to happen in person, so we put a lot of thought into retreats, offsites, and other ways to gather.

#7168. Engineering Manager - Pipelines at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We're looking for an Engineering Manager to lead the Pipelines team at Supabase. Supabase Pipelines is a managed change data capture product. It copies the tables you select from Supabase Postgres to other systems, then streams later changes. The product is in public alpha, with BigQuery, ClickHouse, DuckLake and Snowflake destinations.

Pipelines uses Postgres logical replication and Supabase ETL, our open source replication engine written in Rust. The team works close to the database. That means replication slots, WAL retention, initial table syncs, and the load they place on a customer's source database. The team also builds and runs the destination connectors. Correctness, ordering and recovery matter there as much as speed.

The Engineering Manager is a people, delivery and technical role. We don't expect you to write production code. We do expect you to join design discussions and help set the bar for engineering quality, testing and operations.

Build a healthy, high-trust team culture in a remote-first, globally distributed environment.

Run 1:1s, performance management, career development, and onboarding for your direct reports.

Handle people admin (leave, time off, etc.) for your team.

Own the hiring plan for your team and act as Hiring Manager. First round of interviewer for all roles on the team.

Partner with Talent Acquisition to source, interview, and close strong candidates.

Contribute to headcount planning, org design, and the evolution of engineering leadership at Supabase.

Own the team's roadmap and project management (Linear), driving accountability and clear milestones.

Manage workflow intake and Slack triage. These are the team’s primary inboxes for cross-team requests. You decide what gets routed where, prioritized, or pushed back on.

Own the team's weekly updates and keep stakeholders aligned without creating overhead.

Organize effort against support tickets and maintain the team's SLO.

Surface risks, dependencies, and tradeoffs early and clearly to leadership.

Train as an Incident Commander. Drive coordination, resolution, and post-incident follow-up.

Have 5+ years of engineering background, with enough depth to be credible with engineers building complex systems.

Have 3+ years managing remote engineering teams.

Have managed a team that built and ran data pipelines, change data capture, replication or streaming systems.

Have strong technical credibility in data systems. We look for delivery guarantees, ordering, idempotency, schema changes and backfills.

Know Postgres replication well, including logical replication, the WAL and replication slots, and how they affect a production database.

Know analytical destinations such as BigQuery, Snowflake, ClickHouse or lakehouse table formats like Iceberg.

Have led a team through production incidents on a system that holds customer data. You ran the follow-up work and kept the review blameless.

Have shipped a developer-facing data product to customers outside your company.

Have experience with Rust (not required but a plus)

Have managed remote, globally distributed teams that work asynchronously across disparate time zones.

Are technical enough to be credible with strong engineers, but genuinely passionate about people management. This is the work you want to do, not a track you're settling for.

Build psychological safety and a feedback culture. You give feedback often and in the moment, ask for it back, and create space for the team to do the same.

Operate as a multiplier. You help individuals achieve more than they could alone, and you don't need to be the best technical voice in the room.

Coach rather than direct. You remove blockers, connect people to context, and trust your engineers to own how the work gets done.

Have a real track record of performance management and can speak to specific examples of difficult conversations.

Are comfortable with ambiguity and can build structure where there isn't much.

Communicate clearly and write well. You default to async and written, set context early, and surface risks early.

Are collaborative, low-ego and adaptable. You thrive with high-autonomy senior engineers who don't need you to be the smartest person in the room.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

#7165. Engineering Manager - Realtime at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We're looking for an Engineering Manager to lead the Realtime team at Supabase. Realtime lets developers send low-latency messages between clients, track and synchronize user state, and listen to database changes. Those three features are Broadcast, Presence and Postgres Changes. Broadcast also sends from the database and over HTTP, and clients can replay recent messages.

Realtime is a globally distributed Elixir cluster built on Phoenix. The team balances low latency, fair limits between projects, and the cost of running the service. Customers expect connections to stay up, so reliability matters as much as new features.

The Engineering Manager is a people, delivery and technical role. We don't expect you to write production code, but you you will lead a team of experienced engineers. We do expect you to join design discussions and help set the bar for engineering quality, testing and operations.

Build a healthy, high-trust team culture in a remote-first, globally distributed environment.

Run 1:1s, performance management, career development, and onboarding for your direct reports.

Handle people admin (leave, time off, etc.) for your team.

Own the hiring plan for your team and act as Hiring Manager. First round of interviewer for all roles on the team.

Partner with Talent Acquisition to source, interview, and close strong candidates.

Contribute to headcount planning, org design, and the evolution of engineering leadership at Supabase.

Own the team's roadmap and project management (Linear), driving accountability and clear milestones.

Manage workflow intake and Slack triage. These are the team’s primary inboxes for cross-team requests. You decide what gets routed where, prioritized, or pushed back on.

Own the team's weekly updates and keep stakeholders aligned without creating overhead.

Organize effort against support tickets and maintain the team's SLO.

Surface risks, dependencies, and tradeoffs early and clearly to leadership.

Train as an Incident Commander. Drive coordination, resolution, and post-incident follow-up.

Have 5+ years of engineering background, with enough depth to be credible with engineers building complex systems.

Have 3+ years managing remote engineering teams.

Have managed a team that built and ran a low-latency, high-connection service. Examples are WebSockets, pub/sub, messaging, presence or streaming systems.

Have strong technical credibility in distributed systems. We look for clustering, rate limiting, backpressure and capacity planning.

Have managed remote, globally distributed teams that work asynchronously across disparate time zones.

Have a real track record of performance management and can speak to specific examples of difficult conversations.

Are comfortable with ambiguity and can build structure where there isn't much.

#7164. Target’s New AI Assistant Is Just For Browsing, For Now

While retailers have been developing agents that can shop on behalf of customers, Target’s new shopping assistant is not that—yet.

In October, Target rolled out a beta version of its AI assistant, Ask Target, within its website and mobile app. The assistant finds items based on shopper preferences, shares product facts, compares items, summarizes reviews, and checks inventory availability.

Understand AI/ML infrastructure requirements, including high-density power and advanced cooling systems

Have had leadership positions (General Superintendent, Project Manager) within a GC or EPC on hyperscale data center projects or in self performance of hyperscale data center development projects.

Prioritize extreme ownership and have the ability to create winning cultures across diverse job sites.

Are deeply familiar with lease negotiations, construction contracts, or real estate development processes and have strategies to move those arrangements from traditionally transaction and risk managed arrangements to collaborative efforts that align incentives around accelerated delivery of facilities

Minimum education: Bachelor’s degree or an equivalent combination of education, training, and/or experience

Required field of study: A field relevant to the role as demonstrated through coursework, training, or professional experience

Minimum years of experience: Years of experience required will correlate with the internal job level requirements for the position

Location-based hybrid policy: Currently, we expect all staff to be in one of our offices at least 25% of the time. However, some roles may require more time in our offices.

Visa sponsorship: We do sponsor visas! However, we aren't able to successfully sponsor visas for every role and every candidate. But if we make you an offer, we will make every reasonable effort to get you a visa, and we retain an immigration lawyer to help with this.

For roles performing work in California: We will consider for employment all qualified applicants, including those with arrest or conviction records or criminal histories, in a manner consistent with the requirements of applicable state and local laws, including the San Francisco Fair Chance Ordinance, the City of Los Angeles’ Fair Chance Initiative for Hiring Ordinance, the Los Angeles County Fair Chance Ordinance for Employers, and the California Fair Chance Act.

We encourage you to apply even if you do not believe you meet every single qualification. Not all strong candidates will meet every single qualification as listed.  Research shows that people who identify as being from underrepresented groups are more prone to experiencing imposter syndrome and doubting the strength of their candidacy, so we urge you not to exclude yourself prematurely and to submit an application if you're interested in this work. We think AI systems like the ones we're building have enormous social and ethical implications. We think this makes representation even more important, and we strive to include a range of diverse perspectives on our team.Your safety matters to us. To protect yourself from potential scams, remember that Anthropic recruiters only contact you from @anthropic.com email addresses. In some cases, we may partner with vetted recruiting agencies who will identify themselves as working on behalf of Anthropic. Be cautious of emails from other domains. Legitimate Anthropic recruiters will never ask for money, fees, or banking information before your first day. If you're ever unsure about a communication, don't click any links—visit anthropic.com/careers directly for confirmed position openings.

We believe that the highest-impact AI research will be big science. At Anthropic we work as a single cohesive team on just a few large-scale research efforts. And we value impact — advancing our long-term goals of steerable, trustworthy AI — rather than work on smaller and more specific puzzles. We view AI research as an empirical science, which has as much in common with physics and biology as with traditional efforts in computer science. We're an extremely collaborative group, and we host frequent research discussions to ensure that we are pursuing the highest-impact work at any given time. As such, we greatly value communication skills.

The easiest way to understand our research directions is to read our recent research. This research continues many of the directions our team worked on prior to Anthropic, including: GPT-3, Circuit-Based Interpretability, Multimodal Neurons, Scaling Laws, AI & Compute, Concrete Problems in AI Safety, and Learning from Human Preferences.

Anthropic is a public benefit corporation headquartered in San Francisco. We offer competitive compensation and benefits, optional equity donation matching, generous vacation and parental leave, flexible working hours, and a lovely office space in which to collaborate with colleagues. Guidance on Candidates' AI Usage: Learn about our policy for using AI in our application process.

No forms. Just smart, optimized applications.
