#7147. Public Policy Lead - UK at Kindred

Kindred is a members-only home swapping platform that unlocks the ability to live a travel-rich lifestyle through the power of community. By exchanging primary residences with trusted peers, renters and owners alike can match with Kindred spirits and explore new destinations without breaking the bank.

Kindred is revolutionizing the “third option” of travel accommodation, compared to short-term rentals and hotels. Since launching in 2022, Kindred has grown to a global community of 300,000 members across 150+ cities and puts home swapping on the map as a radically more affordable, responsible, and connected way to travel.

To support that momentum, Kindred has raised over $150M in capital from some of the most credible investors in Silicon Valley, including Index Ventures, Andreessen Horowitz, New Enterprise Associates, Bessemer Venture Partners, Caffeinated Capital, Elad Gil, and founders of companies like Opendoor, Figma, ClassPass, and more.

Kindred’s co-founders are proven leaders from the early team at Opendoor ($15B+ exit), who have each built and scaled products that today generate $1B+ annual revenue combined.

We’re looking for the world’s top builders, executors, and believers to help us make travel more meaningful.

You can learn more about us in Forbes and The New York Times.

This is Kindred’s first dedicated public policy hire. You’ll join a three-person legal and policy team (reporting to the General Counsel) and own the execution of Kindred’s public policy and government relations efforts, with a focus on European markets but covering additional markets as necessary.

The role is primarily focused on proactive advocacy to shape the policy environment for home-swapping. You’ll build relationships with elected officials, regulators, and industry groups. You’ll commission and manage economic impact research. You’ll develop city-level partnerships that normalize home-swapping as a category distinct from other travel categories. You’ll also develop working familiarity with the tax, housing, and accommodation regulations that intersect with Kindred’s model, and help parse how those frameworks apply (or don’t) to credit-based home-swapping. You’ll do all of this initially as a team of one, with outside consultants to support you and a path to building a team as the company grows and the function matures.

Execute Kindred’s public policy strategy with an emphasis on proactive advocacy.

Build and maintain relationships with elected officials, regulators, and staff at the local, state/provincial, and national levels. Represent Kindred in meetings, hearings, coalition convenings, and industry events.

Track proposed housing, short-term rental, tax, and platform regulations that could impact Kindred’s model, identifying where Kindred’s credit-based, non-commercial structure creates meaningful regulatory distinctions. Use AI-assisted tools and other technology to monitor developments across jurisdictions.

Further develop and evolve Kindred’s core policy narrative and positioning materials. Draft position papers, comment letters, testimony, and advocacy materials.

Propose and negotiate city-level partnerships that promote and normalize home-swapping. Build relationships with housing advocacy organizations, tourism boards, trade associations, and aligned industry coalitions.

Commission and manage third-party economic analyses on the impact of Kindred’s model, collaborating with internal business teams and outside consulting firms.

Identify, evaluate, and manage outside consultants cost-effectively (lobbyists, policy advisors, research firms).

Partner with internal teams to develop externally facing materials and data packages for stakeholder engagements.

Coordinate with the General Counsel on lobbying compliance, political activity disclosures, and regulatory filings.

7–10 years of experience in public policy, with at least 3 years at a technology company or similar fast paced environment.

Experience building or meaningfully contributing to building a public policy or government relations function, not just operating within an established one.

Track record of productive relationships with elected officials, regulators, and policy stakeholders.

Experience managing outside consultants, lobbyists, or policy advisors across multiple jurisdictions.

Strong written and verbal communication skills, including experience drafting policy position papers, advocacy materials, or regulatory comments.

Experience with policy matters in Europe, with an interest in policy work in new jurisdictions.

Fluency with AI tools and a willingness to integrate them into policy monitoring, research, and content development workflows.

Willingness to operate as an individual contributor while building the function, with the judgment to know when to engage outside support.

Experience in or adjacent to the sharing economy, home-sharing, travel, housing, or short-term rental sectors.

Experience at a growth-stage company (under 500 employees).

Familiarity with vacation/short-term rental, housing, and land use regulations, as well as related tax regulations.

Comfort with detailed regulatory analysis: ability to read and interpret statutes, ordinances, and administrative rules, and translate their implications into actionable policy positions. A law degree and legal practice experience is a plus but not required.

Build something new: You’ll help define the function and shape how Kindred engages with governments and regulators from the ground up.

A genuinely different model: Kindred’s credit-based, non-commercial home-swapping model is structurally distinct from existing travel lodging categories, contributing to sustainable tourism objectives.

Path to leadership: As the company and function grow, this role offers the opportunity to greater leadership.

Competitive compensation: Base plus meaningful equity in a growth-stage company.

At Kindred, we know that good things happen when we look out for one another. We offer a thoughtful, human-first benefits package designed to support health, wellbeing, and connection.Our openings may span more than one career level. The base salary offered depends on factors such as experience, transferable skills, business needs, impact, and market data.

Private health insurance, pension contributions, and enhanced parental leave

25 days of annual leave, plus 8 paid bank holidays

21 credits per year to stay in Kindred homes

£3,200 annual travel stipend to help offset travel-related costs

Remote-first and remote-flexible, with a £400 annual budget to meet up with teammates and a yearly company-paid trip to Kindred HQ in San Francisco

Regular team and company offsites to connect, collaborate, and build together

A four-week paid sabbatical after five years with Kindred

A vibrant, inclusive, and highly skilled team that ferociously protects team chemistry

A culture of high standards, feedback, and continual growth

#7145. Brandweek 2026: Consumer-First Still Beats AI-First ft. Laura Knebusch

AI can raise the ceiling on marketing, or it can expose every weak foundation underneath it.

Live from the Marketing Vanguard stage at Brandweek 2026, Jenny Rooney sits down with Laura Knebusch, senior vice president of CPG marketing and customer experience at Georgia-Pacific, to explore what modern brand building really requires when media, data, retail, and AI are all evolving at once.

Laura gets into why Georgia-Pacific spent 18 months consolidating data sources, and how that foundation now supports in-house capabilities and agentic AI.

She also discusses why consumer-first and AI-first marketing are not actually competing ideas.

This episode is part of a special vidcast series that was recorded live during Brandweek 2026 and presented in partnership with Tatari.

Laura Knebusch is Senior Vice President of CPG Marketing and Customer Experience at Georgia-Pacific, where her team supports consumer and B2B brands across insights, content, media, promotions, marketing technology and analytics.

She began her marketing career at Procter & Gamble after earning her MBA from Duke and later joined Georgia-Pacific, where she has spent 18 years across brand management, category leadership and marketing capability roles.

Today, her focus is on combining classic brand-building fundamentals with the data, technology and operating models needed for modern CPG marketing.

Private Label Is Waiting, So What About Your Brand? — Laura points out that consumers have been adjusting their behavior for years, not reacting to one sudden economic moment. In household essentials, private-label options are credible and easy to access. There’s a warning lying there for marketers: if the brand does not create a real reason to choose it, loyalty becomes much harder to defend.

100 Data Sources, One Source of Truth, Eighteen Months of Work — When Laura stepped into her current role, Georgia-Pacific needed to become more digitally capable. The first step was not another tool. It was fixing the data. Over 18 months, the team connected roughly 100 different sources, cleaned and harmonized them, and built one accessible system that now powers multiple in-house capabilities.

Consumer-First vs. AI-First Is the Wrong Debate — At Brandweek 2026, Laura describes an internal discussion that paired behavioral science with generative-engine optimization. At first, the advice sounded different – Humans need simplicity, distinctiveness and repetition, while AI needs clear, consistent information. But the conclusion was the same: strong fundamentals work for both.

Why Georgia-Pacific Merged Shopper Marketing and National Media — Retail media has changed how consumers discover and buy, so Georgia-Pacific changed how it organizes around that reality. Laura explains why shopper marketing and national media were brought closer together, helping the team better understand where partners create strategic value and where investment should stay tactical.

Jenny Rooney is Chief Brand and Community Officer, leading strategy for the overall ADWEEK brand as well as the ways in which we serve and support our audiences with high-value content, products, partnerships and experiences, notably through our community programs such as Marketing Vanguard.

Select ADWEEK as one of your favorite sources and stay up to date on our latest content.

Beyond flying 100 inflatables and bringing on new brand partners, Macy's is airing a love letter to its history. CMO Sharon Otterman explains why

The ACC Network agreement includes a presenting sponsorship of ACC Huddle Final Score

#7143. Senior Product Marketing Manager - Scaling Creators at Kit

Kit is the email marketing platform experts choose to grow their audience, deepen their relationships, and build a business around the work they love. For coaches, YouTubers, authors, podcasters, and other creatives, there isn't a better marketing hub to rely on to grow audiences, automate email marketing, and sell digital products — all within one platform.More importantly, there isn't a team more committed to helping creators earn a living. We're on a mission to help creators earn $1 billion using our creator marketing platform. We have always been 100% independent and 100% remote.We are proud to have built a product that our customers love, and we look for people who have enthusiasm and belief in our mission, vision, and values to join our team. We're also embracing AI thoughtfully — both in how we build and how we hire to ensure our team is adaptable, innovative, and ready for what's next.

Kit's most established creators run real companies, with teams, large lists, dedicated sending needs, and a buying process that our self-serve motion wasn't built to serve. Because this part of our business is growing, we're looking for a Senior Product Marketing Manager, Scaling Creators to own how Kit wins, lands, and expands these businesses through a sales-assisted motion that sits alongside our self-serve product.

You'll define who this buyer is, write the story that resonates and wins with them, and build the GTM strategy to drive inbound pipeline for our sales team. When these creators choose Kit and grow on it, they build more valuable businesses, and that's the outcome this role exists for.

This is a full-time individual contributor role ideal for someone who has added an upmarket sales-led motion to a product-led company before and wants their work measured in win rate and deal size.

You'll report to Christina Lhi, Head of Marketing, and work alongside two other experienced Product Marketing Managers on Kit's product marketing team. Together, you'll keep the self-serve and sales-assisted stories feeling like one Kit.

Our sales leadership, on who we sell to, the deal toolkit, enablement, and win/loss

Account Executives and Solutions Consultants, on demo narratives, discovery, and technical and migration proof

Product, on packaging, launches, and roadmap input

RevOps and analytics, on how accounts get routed to sales, attribution, and pricing decisions

Creator Success, on case studies, customer references, and expansion plays

Demand gen, content, and events, on campaigns and events for established creators

First Week: Dive into our onboarding curriculum in Notion and learn Kit's product, pricing, and current positioning. Attend Get-To-Know-You (GTKY) meetings with marketing, sales, Solutions Consultants, RevOps, and Creator Success. Get access to our CRM, call recordings, and product analytics, and start listening to recent sales calls. Understand how product marketing currently works at Kit

First Month: Shadow 15+ sales calls and talk with 10 established creators, including recent wins, losses, and churned accounts. Audit the sales content reps use today and how product usage currently routes accounts to sales. Set baselines with RevOps for win rate, sales cycle, deal size, and expansion revenue. Share a written point of view with the entire growth team on who our established creator buyer is and the three biggest gaps in how we sell to them. And an action plan on how we can win these creators.

First Year: Sales runs on messaging and tools you built, pitch with confidence and we drive 2X inbound MQLs from when you started. You’ll own and lead our channel strategy, setting the vision, driving cross-functional alignment, and partnering with Marketing to bring it to life. You'll run a quarterly win/loss program and share what you learn openly, build a clear path for sales leads on our website, and make sure launches that matter to larger accounts ship with both a self-serve and a sales-assisted version. Your work has a direct impact on helping Kit reach $100,000,000 ARR while protecting the self-serve engine that got us here.

Segmentation and buyer definition: turning firmographic and product usage data into a clear picture of who to sell to and when

Messaging and positioning for a sales-assisted buyer, including competitive differentiation and objection handling

Sales enablement that reps actually use: demo narratives, discovery guides, business cases, and battlecards

Writing and storytelling that turns product depth into a business case a non-technical buyer can act on

Working with CRM, product analytics, and pipeline data to measure funnel conversion, win rate, and deal size

Customer and market/competitor research, including win/loss interviews and call analysis

Systems thinking: connecting people, processes, data, and tools to identify opportunities and build repeatable solutions across the go-to-market motion

Strong AI fluency: knowing when to leverage AI and how to build AI-powered workflows or tools that scale product marketing work, with human judgment and quality checks built in

Pricing and packaging input for a new tier or add-on (nice to have)

7+ years in B2B SaaS product marketing, including at least 2 years supporting a sales team selling to mid-market and/or larger accounts

Added a sales-led motion on top of a product-led one at a high-growth company, and can show what changed before and after with clear metrics

Built a deal toolkit and enablement program from little or nothing, and tied it to a sales metric like win rate or deal size

Ran a win/loss or customer research program that changed how a team sold or built

Ran an expansion or account-based program for an existing customer base (nice to have)

Worked in email, martech, or the creator economy

You lead without a formal title. You align sales, product, RevOps, and finance on goals, trade-offs, and what success looks like, then keep things moving.

You advocate for creators in decisions, and you protect the self-serve experience instead of pushing every account toward sales.

You act with urgency and stay focused on hitting OKRs and owning outcomes.

You work in public. You share findings, drafts, and the reasoning behind decisions so others can build on them.

You connect data to decisions, and you help others get sharper about using it.

You raise the bar on quality and iterate toward it, and you shape how your team uses AI, from call analysis to keeping battlecards current.

Experience in only self-serve or only enterprise sales. This role lives in the space between them, and you'll need to understand both.

Treating enablement as a deck handoff. The work includes coaching reps, measuring what gets used, and revising what doesn't land.

Wanting a sales playbook already in place. Much of this motion doesn't exist yet, and you'll be building it as you learn.

Preferring to be measured on output rather than outcomes. You'll share accountability with Sales for win rate, deal size, and expansion revenue.

Needing extensive approval before acting. You'll be trusted to make calls on speed versus quality and to explain your reasoning.

Discomfort with direct, candid feedback, especially from sellers on what's working in live deals.

We're 100% remote with team members across the globe. We work mostly on East Coast time—so if you're based on the West Coast of the United States, Europe, or Asia, you might have a few early mornings or late evenings. We do our best to keep things reasonable, but a little time zone juggling comes with the territory.

Kit has standardized salaries based on position, no matter where you live. For this role, we’re hiring at our level 4 ($166,000). Level is determined based on experience and our interview process.

We cover up to $2,280 per month toward medical premiums, with dental and vision premiums fully covered. We offer Health Insurance plans through Aetna

$2,000 equipment allowance for your first two years, $1,000 budget every following two years. Company-provided laptops are issued to every Kit team member and are not included in the equipment budget

Individual learning + development budget ($3,500/year)

Creator meetup budget ($50/month) to connect with creators over coffee, lunch, or other in-person meetups

Childcare benefit up to $3,000 annually through JOON

Twenty (20) days of paid time off during each year of employment

Paid paid vacation: An after-tax bonus of $1,000 for taking five consecutive days of vacation where you’re fully unplugged from work

Two weeks of paid sick time each year, including mental health + well being days

Twelve (12) weeks paid parental leave and flexible scheduling in your child’s first year

Up to six weeks of paid bereavement leave, medical leave, and disaster after six months of employment, two weeks of each paid leave in your first six months

Winter Break Closure: Kit closes for a week at the end of December, giving everyone a collective break to enjoy the holiday season. Essential support services remain available, with teams coordinating to ensure coverage during this period

Four-week, paid sabbatical after five years with the team

Fantastic in-person or virtual retreats with the team twice a year

We usually receive a large number of applications, and our goal is to follow up with you within two weeks. You’ll hear from us either way. Here’s what you can expect from the rest of our hiring process:

A phone screen with our recruiter (30 min)

We’ll keep you informed through every step. To get started, complete this application, including a cover letter and answers to the questions on the next page. We read every single application, and your cover letter and responses help put your experience into context.

We value diversity in all of its forms. Research tells us that applicants who are female or non-binary, as well as applicants who are people of color, are less likely to apply for roles they do not feel 100% qualified for. If you think you meet more than 50% of our requirements but fewer than 100% of them, please apply.

We’re imperfect communicators, so think of our job postings as the starting point for discussion rather than proof that you shouldn’t apply.

Kit does not discriminate on the basis of race, sex, color, religion, age, national origin, marital status, disability, veteran status, genetic information, sexual orientation, gender identity or any other reason prohibited by law in provision of employment opportunities and benefits.

#7140. Week of Nov 16 Evening News Ratings: NBC Nightly News Is Only Newscast with Gains

NBC Nightly News with Tom Llamas finished the week of November 16 as the only broadcast with growth in both total viewers and the Adults 25-54 demo.

Nightly News’ performance was even more impressive in the prized demo, narrowing the viewership gap with ABC World News Tonight with David Muir to just 29,000 viewers.

Note: The weekly averages of ABC World News Tonight, CBS Evening News, and NBC Nightly News are based on four days (Monday-Thursday). Friday’s (11/20/2026) ABC News’ broadcast was retitled to WNT-ABC, CBS Evening News and NBC Nightly News were retitled to CBS Evening Nws, and NBC Nitely News, respectively. These broadcasts are excluded from the weekly and season averages.

According to Nielsen national live+same-day big data and program ratings, ABC World News Tonight averaged 9.09 million total viewers and 1.267 million Adults 25-54 demo viewers for the week of Nov. 16. The evening newscast was down 2% in total viewers and 7% in the A25-54 viewers compared to the previous week. Compared to the same week in 2025 (the week beginning Nov. 17), WNT was up 14% in total viewers and 27% in the demo.

NBC Nightly News took the No. 2 spot in total viewers and the demo, averaging 7.366 million and 1.238 million viewers, respectively. Compared to the previous week, Nightly News grew by 3% in total viewers and 7% in the demo. Looking at its performance to the same week a year ago, Nightly News was up by 14% in total viewers and 43% in the demo.

CBS Evening News closed out the week of Nov. 16 with 4.628 million total viewers and 650,000 demo viewers. CBS News’ newscast was flat in total viewers and down 6% in the demo compared to the previous week. Year-ago week numbers showed CBS Evening News up 12% in total viewers and 23% in the demo.

#7134. Want to Sell an Agency? Launch a Creator Marketing Firm

This story was originally published in On Background with Mark Stenberg, a free weekly newsletter that explores the key themes shaping the media industry. You can sign up for it here.

In recent months, the market for creator and social agencies has been, to put it lightly, brisk.

In September, the Madrid-based social agency Samy acquired Get Engaged, a U.S. social and influencer shop, in a deal valued in the low nine figures.

In October, Billion Dollar Boy Group bought Creator Authority, a business-to-business influencer agency, and the private equity firm Shamrock Capital, which owns ADWEEK, made an eight-figure investment in the social-first agency Saylor.

Earlier this month, the social-first agency LV8 merged with its sister company Mirage Digital on the back of a “high eight figures” investment from Velocity Capital Management. And last week, the private equity firm Falfurrias Management Partners took a majority stake in Freeman & Forrest, another B2B creator agency.

The deals are part of a record year, according to James Creech, founder of the creator M&A advisory firm Quartermast. There have been 30 agency acquisitions in the creator economy so far in 2026, up from 20 in all of 2025 and above the previous high of 28 in 2022, according to Quartermast data.

Overall, there have been 124 M&A creator-adjacent deals this year, the busiest on record, and roughly half of the agency deals have come since July, per Quartermast.

Behind the surge is a crop of boutiques founded during the pandemic-era influencer boom that have matured into businesses worth buying, which are run by founders who need more resources to keep up with what clients are asking for.

A specific set of firms—social-first creative shops, podcast agencies, TikTok Shop specialists, and B2B creator plays—are responsible for the majority of this interest, while the deals themselves are increasingly financed by a new cohort of buyers, including private equity firms, strategic roll-ups, and international firms. All of it is unfolding in a frothy market for creator economy deals, which is just now beginning to consolidate in response to urgent demand across a fragmented space.

“Creator marketing works,” Creech said. “Brands and their agency partners are going to continue to invest there.”

The current rush of creator agency activity has its origins in the pandemic, according to LV8 chief executive Griffin Haddrill.

Lockdown acted as a digital accelerant, pulling budgets toward creators at a moment when television and film had dried up and out-of-home advertising had stopped working.

Because acquisitions typically take nine to 12 months to come together, the resulting deal volume spiked in 2021 and 2022, slowed over the following two years, and has since normalized, Creech said.

The same period produced a wave of new agencies, many of which were founded by executives who saw the emerging relevance of the creator ecosystem and launched startups to service it. Five years later, those boutiques have matured into businesses worth buying, according to Haddrill.

FOR Scoops, Sharp Analysis, and Insider Context

For founders, selling offers the resources to keep up with clients, according to Get Engaged co-founder Ben Hiott.

“Social is becoming the center of how brands build their businesses, and clients are asking us to do more every year,” Hiott said in October.

The wave of dealmaking that followed the pandemic was primarily influencer agencies, according to Creech, while this year, the variety of targets has expanded.

In 2026, buyers have gone after social-first creative agencies, such as Get Engaged, OK Cool, and Sway Social; podcast and audio shops like Ad Results Media and Listening Party Agency; and TikTok Shop specialists including Orca, Uptickk, and Yada.

Private equity is also getting more involved in the space, investing capital after years of financing strategic acquirers, as Bridgepoint does with Samy, according to Creech. This year, Shamrock Capital took a majority stake in Saylor, Velocity became a near-equal partner in LV8, and Falfurrias bought Freeman & Forrest, among other deals.

Serial acquirers are consolidating the field as well. Influencer agency Ykone has made four acquisitions in 2026, and creator company network Residence has made two, while Samy and Publicis remain active, according to Creech. After large platform deals for Influential and Captiv8, Publicis has turned to regional acquisitions, including the French creator agency Point d’Orgue, to enter new markets, according to Creech.

For investors, the opportunity lies in stitching these shops together, according to Haddrill.

“The consumer demand was there, but the agency space was fragmented,” Haddrill said. “Now, investors are seeing this fragmentation and working to bring them together.”

B2B creator marketing has also emerged as its own category.

Creator Authority, whose clients include Amazon, Adobe, and Mastercard, was Billion Dollar Boy Group’s first acquisition, an all-cash, multimillion-dollar deal with an earnout component, according to Business Insider.

Freeman & Forrest, founded in 2024 by Emily Freeman and Forrest Brazeal after they ran influencer programs at Microsoft and Google Cloud, helps tech companies like Amazon and Google reach technical customers through creators.

And more of the deals cross borders: Get Engaged gave Samy a U.S. base after the agency built its business in Europe and Latin America, and dealmaking between the U.S. and the U.K. has picked up in both directions, according to Creech.

As the ecosystem matures, the next wave of dealmaking may come from the middle of the market, according to a creator management executive who asked not to be named.

The largest independents, such as Whalar and Influential, have already sold to Accenture Song and Publicis, respectively, which leaves room for midsize independents, many of which will come to market over the next 12 months, the executive said.

Creator management firms, which run creators’ businesses rather than simply booking their brand deals, are likely the next category to consolidate, the executive added.

The buying, in other words, is far from over, according to Creech.

“It’s mature, it’s scaled, it’s professionalized. The work is not done,” Creech said. “We’re maybe entering middle innings.”

Hearst CMO Heads Out (SCOOP): The chief marketing officer at Hearst Magazines, Sherri Chambers, will be leaving the organization in a few weeks, according to sources. Chambers, who joined the digital media firm in December 2024, confirmed the news, while a Hearst Magazines spokesperson expressed their gratitude for Chambers in a statement. The departure is the latest in a series of executive shake-ups. In July, Hearst Magazines appointed Chris Berend as its first chief content and experience officer, and in October, it named A+E executive Juliana Stock as its new president, replacing Debi Chirichella, who will become chair of the group. The legacy media firm has worked hard to cultivate a reputation for calm and consistency in an otherwise turbulent industry, but it certainly looks as if the company is aiming to turn some kind of corner.

Bunny Boss Bolts: The editor in chief of Playboy, Phillip Picardi, has left the organization after less than a year, Puck reported earlier this week. According to a company spokesperson, the move was a personal decision, with Picardi returning to Los Angeles after Playboy set up headquarters in—where else—Miami. But Picardi only began the job officially in March, meaning his tenure at the publisher only lasted a brief eight months. Given all the fanfare surrounding his appointment, I have to imagine something went seriously awry. Or Picardi, who did not return my request for comment, landed an otherwise irrefusable job elsewhere. This is disappointing because I liked the direction Playboy was heading under Picardi, reporting on the state of sex, dating, and relationships in this fraught moment—but maybe the loyal fanbase did not.

BuzzFeed Taps Influential (EXCLUSIVE): On Monday, BuzzFeed named its first chief revenue officer under its new ownership, tapping Chris Poydenis, co-founder of the creator marketing firm Influential, to lead its revenue division. The appointment comes as BuzzFeed attempts an ambitious shift in direction, positioning itself as a source of free, ad-supported television, bundled with the rest of the Allen Media Group portfolio. The decision to name a creator marketing executive as the chief sales lead clearly telegraphs the manner in which BuzzFeed would like to evolve, shifting focus to its creators and video franchise talent. Will it work? The odds are long.

Sara Kehaulani Goo is president of The Washington Post’s creator network, a new division that sits outside of the newsroom and makes sponsored short-form video series with independent creators. Goo, formerly the editor in chief of Axios, launched the first show in April, and the network has since released six series in categories ranging from personal finance to career advice.

Publishers increasingly want to work with creators, but the harder question is why creators would want to work with publishers. In our conversation, Goo and I discussed how The Post’s model works, why its creator content goes through Post fact-checking, and what a media company can offer talent who could go it alone.

Mark Stenberg: You specifically left Axios to head up this new creator division. Can you tell me about the role and how The Post is working with creators?

Sara Kehaulani Goo: I spent 12 years of my career at The Post as a reporter and editor, so it’s been nice to return in a very different role. The theory is simple: Look at where people spend their time, where they get information, where trust is growing, and where revenue is growing across media. The answer to all of those questions is creators. So, we are working with knowledge creators, people who have built big audiences around their expertise, and treating them as equal editorial and business partners.

Sara: We mostly work with creators who’ve built audiences with short-form video in lifestyle topics like personal finance, health and wellness, and career advice. Then, we develop the shows together and publish them together. Last week, we launched Her Next Move with an executive recruiter based in New York. She posts a two-minute video to her audience of a million people as a collab with us, so it reaches both audiences. We amplify it on The Post’s social accounts and publish it on our website and app. That show is sponsored by Uber.

Mark: Why would a creator want to work with the Post? Do they share in the revenue?

Sara: I won’t get into the details, but we pay them for their time. It’s similar to a contributor model, though not exactly like freelancing, because they’re entrepreneurs. A lot of them want to focus on the content, which is what journalists want too. We help build the business around them, and we provide distribution and editorial support when they need it.

Mark: Does their content go through Post fact-checking?

Sara: Yes, through my team. The standards are the same. There’s a lack of consistency in the creator world when it comes to disclosing sponsorships, and to quality in general, and we can bring that. Trust in creators is growing, but people still wonder whether they should trust what they’re watching on Instagram and who paid for it. We can help answer that.

Mark: It’s early, but how big is this now, and where is it headed?

Sara: This year has been a proof of concept. We’ve done six shows with six creators, across health and wellness, personal finance, travel, career advice, and entertainment. Now we’re deciding which ones to renew, and next year we want to do more always-on content. Brands are very interested, because every brand is thinking about its creator strategy, and finding the right creator takes a lot of effort. We take that headache off their shoulders.

Mark: Creators often relish operating on their own. How do you convince them to work with you?

Sara: Having led newsrooms, I’ve always seen my job as spotting talent, developing it, and creating an environment where people can do their best work. This is the same. Creators are entrepreneurs, and the platforms give them plenty of ways to make money on their own. But being an entrepreneur isn’t for everybody, at least not at every level. Media companies have always worked with outside experts. The structure just looks different now.

#7128. 5 Black Friday Truths About Gen Z Shoppers and the Mall

Welcome to Black Friday—the start of the most critical five days of the year for retail brands.

According to Circana, 43% of Americans will do most of their holiday shopping between today and Monday, and nearly a quarter of them will do most of it today, full stop.

This is also the day that brands are focused on Gen Z, because 94% of them plan to shop this week, according to Deloitte, and they’ll spend an average of $752—more than any other age cohort.

It’s a given that many consumers aged 14-29 will spend that money online. But a lot of shopping is also going to happen at the place Gen X made famous: the mall.

You’ll find plenty of zoomers in malls today, in fact, but they’re not all spending the way you think, and some aren’t there to shop at all. ADWEEK waded through a stack of recent market studies and tapped the shoulders of a few experts to plumb Gen Z’s current relationship with the mall and what it means for Black Friday 2026.

Gen X might have been the original mall rats in the 1980s, but today’s 18-29 age group has picked up the mantle.

According to Coresight research released in April, 48% of Gen Z consumers say they’ve hit the mall in the last two weeks. A recent Ipsos survey found that shoppers aged 18-34 were twice as likely as those 55 and up to shop at the mall. And according to a Stagwell study released this year, Gen Z not only spends more time at the mall on a per-visit basis, they also spend $296 per visit on average, the most of any age group.

Gen Z mall affinity seems strongest with women, too. A 2026 study of female zoomers’ shopping activity conducted by snack maker Sunnie and Westfield Rise found that 86% of them praised the mall for giving them “energy they can’t get digitally.”

The reasons why Gen Z likes the mall have as much to do with what the mall is as what it’s not—a small screen. Mall shopping represents a rebound effect in retail form, a response to hours of isolated scrolling.

Coresight’s work states it plainly. “Gen Z has grown up with frictionless digital commerce as a baseline expectation,” researchers wrote, “yet precisely because of this ubiquity, they increasingly seek the tangible, social and experiential dimensions of physical retail.”

It’s worth noting that experts are also starting to see the same dynamic unfolding with Gen Alpha (aged 13 and under), whose spending power and influence is already approaching $100 billion, per Charles Schwab.

“A trip to the mall with friends can be an important first taste of autonomy,” Helenor Gilmour, head of marketing research firm Beano Brain, told ADWEEK. “That shared experience is a powerful part of the appeal of shopping in stores. It is why we expect younger shoppers to embrace shopping events like Black Friday as a reason to visit malls together.”

Brieane Olson, CEO of PacSun, is well positioned to observe the evolving shopping habits of Gen Z. The brand sells its apparel online, in freestanding stores, and at mall locations. It’s easy to assume that a generation that grew up with ecommerce would stick to what they know; or they might defect because they’re tired of it. But the truth lay in between.

“We’re continuing to see just how important physical retail is to young consumers, and there’s a real opportunity for malls heading into Black Friday and the holiday season,” Olson told ADWEEK. “Young people aren’t choosing between digital and physical experiences. They want both, and they expect brands to create meaningful connections across each.”

With over 520 stores across 4.2 million square feet and sales north of $650 million a year, Bloomington, Minnesota’s Mall of America (MOA) is the biggest mall in the continental U.S. According to its 2026 Holiday Shopping Survey, 92% of Gen Z respondents expressed an intent to show up there today, with over half saying they planned to spend once they arrive.

Just as significantly, MOA has charted a symbiotic relationship between the online culture Gen Z consumes and their behavior when they visit.

“Social media may introduce them to a brand, trend or community, but there’s still an appeal to experiencing those interests firsthand,” said a MOA spokesperson. “We’re seeing that with our leasing strategy. Brands such as Edikted and Pop Mart, along with concepts like Bandai Namco and Kpop Nara, reflect how interests that develop online are translating into physical retail experiences.”

“Gen Z may discover what’s next online,” MOA chief business development and marketing officer Jill Renslow added, “but they still want to experience it together in the real world.”

From a brand perspective, it’s not enough that Gen Z merely visits the mall—retailers still need to woo them once they get there.

According to the International Council of Shopping Centers, 60% of Gen Z mall visitors say they go “just to socialize or meet friends.” Sunnie and Westfield North’s work found that 72% of female zoomers would still visit the mall even if it meant they couldn’t buy anything.

But there’s still a marketing opportunity here. Coresight’s study determined that social-media content creation is a big reason for mall visits, calling them “aesthetically curated spaces [that] serve as backdrops for organic and shareable moments.”

The brands that benefit most, said the study, are those that boast “visually engaging environments with Instagrammable decor.”

Robert Klara is the senior editor of brands at Adweek, specializing in the evolution and impact of brands.

Select ADWEEK as one of your favorite sources and stay up to date on our latest content.

#7121. Show Your Work: Platforms Must Make Safety Decisions That Brands Can Verify

This column is adapted from the keynote address that X Head of Safety Michael O’Herlihy gave at the recent Brand and AI Safety Summit.

When I was at school in Ireland, math exams had a rule I thought was deeply unfair. You were allowed to just write down the answer—but if the answer was wrong, you got nothing. Zero.

However, if you showed your work, and your method was right but you’d slipped on one line, you still got partial credit.

It took me years to see the point. Showing your work doesn’t just give people an answer. It helps them understand how you got there. An answer on its own asks for trust; showing the work lets you check it.

I’ve spent two decades in the business of judgment calls: what stays up, what comes down, what is eligible for reach. Every one of those judgments is an answer. And for most of that time, our industry has handed people the answer and kept the work hidden. For too long, this has been a black box.

People ask me who makes better calls, humans or machines. Both make mistakes. If you give the same post to a hundred trained reviewers, you won’t get a hundred identical answers. So “human or machine?” is the wrong question. The right one is: Can you see the work?

Today, mostly, you can’t see inside. The systems that decide what you see, and what sits next to an ad, run out of sight. The deal was “trust us.” It held while volumes were manageable, and it stopped working as they grew.

Here’s the good news. AI doesn’t just make decisions faster. It gives us new ways to make those decisions explainable and testable. We now have the opportunity to turn the black box into a glass box: decisions you can trace back to the rules, and people outside who can check them, challenge them and help us improve them.

But a glass box alone doesn’t make a system safe. You could fill one with bad decisions. It would just let people see them. So transparency on its own isn’t the standard—defensibility is. Can this decision be justified under the rules? Transparency provides the evidence: Can someone see how we got there? Verification is the test: Can someone outside check whether the system consistently follows the rules?

Before returning to X, I worked at Reddit, where my colleague Rosa Català and I published a paper earlier this year called Escaping the Agreement Trap: Defensibility Signals for Evaluating Rule-Governed AI.

The usual test for a content-decision system is whether it agreed with a human. Same answer, pass. Different answer, fail. We looked at more than 193,000 real content decisions from Reddit and asked whether each one could be traced back to the written rules.

When the model and the human disagreed, the usual assumption is that the model got it wrong. But roughly four out of five of those model decisions could still be defended under the written rules. Disagreement doesn’t necessarily mean the model was wrong. Often, both opposing decisions were defensible. Grading by agreement is the wrong exam entirely: checking the answer, never the working.

How can two opposite decisions both be defensible? Ambiguity. Two reasonable people can look at the same case, reach opposite conclusions, and both be within the rules. Some ambiguity isn’t a bug, it’s what happens when real people apply rules to real life. But a lot of it can be managed, and it starts with more detailed rules. When we gave the model detailed written rules instead of just bullets, measured ambiguity dropped from 18% to 7%.

So how do we actually build the glass box? One window at a time.

Window one: See how it’s ranked. We open-sourced the For You algorithm, the system behind the feed: ranking, filtering and visibility-limiting labels. It’s on GitHub, and we keep it updated to match what’s running on X. Anyone can read it; you get the code, not a blog post about the code.

Window two: Add context. That’s Community Notes, where contributors can add context to any post. A note is only shown publicly if enough contributors from different points of view rate it helpful. The algorithm is open, and so is the data. Context comes from the community, not from us.

Window three: See what applies to your account. My colleague Keith Coleman’s team built Under the Hood. It shows account holders the visibility decisions that apply to them, and any restrictions applied due to a government request or court order. You don’t have to guess. You can look.

Window four: Understand the decision. This is what we’re building in Safety. Not just making decisions visible, but giving people clear ways to understand them, challenge them and help us get them right. When we label a post, the author will be able to tap the label and see what it was based on, in plain language, not code or model internals. They’ll see what it means for reach: who it won’t be recommended to, and who will see it behind a warning. And they’ll be able to appeal, telling us what we should have seen, so we can take another look and fix it if we got it wrong.

Sometimes people will still disagree, and that’s fine. Agreement was never the goal. What we want to hear is, “I still disagree, but I see why you got there.” A mistake you can see is a mistake you can fix. But there is a limit.

The glass box shows the rules and the decisions, not the answer key. We don’t publish the signals and thresholds we use to catch spam, scams and coordinated abuse. Open enough to verify. Closed enough that it can’t be gamed.

The past few years have tested advertiser confidence across the industry, and no platform, including X, is exempt from that scrutiny. That skepticism is legitimate, and the right response isn’t to ask for trust back. It’s to make trust earnable. Brands need to understand the decisions that shape where their ads appear, because what’s allowed on a platform and what you want next to your ad are two different standards.

The X platform philosophy of “Freedom of Speech, Not Reach” is built on that distinction. Lawful speech can stay up without being amplified. But limited reach should never mean hidden decisions.

Our rules decide what’s allowed; ranking decides what we recommend. Suitability decides what sits next to a brand, and that’s the brand’s call, not ours. Every brand draws that line differently; advertisers shouldn’t have to accept someone else’s definition of what’s suitable for them.

Control means setting your own standard. Transparency means seeing where your ads appear. Independent measurement means verifying whether it’s working. Published rules tell you what should happen. Transparency shows you what happened.

But neither, on its own, proves that a system is working consistently at scale. Verification does. Platforms shouldn’t be the only ones grading their own work, which is why at X we partner with DoubleVerify and Integral Ad Science.

To the platforms: Show your work. Write rules clear enough that decisions can be traced back to them, and let people outside your walls check the results.

To brands and agencies: Ask for it. Make “Can I check this?” a normal question, and ask every platform, including X: Can I set my own suitability standard? Can I see where my ads actually ran? Who outside your company checks it? Safety shouldn’t be something platforms ask you to trust. It should be something you can verify.

Those math exams had it right. Showing your work gives people the confidence to understand how you got there, and when you get it wrong, it gives them the evidence to say so.

“Trust us” belongs to the black box. It’s time to build the glass box. Show your work.

Michael O'Herlihy is Head of Safety at X, where he leads the work to keep people safe across the platform and its AI systems while protecting free expression and increasing transparency. He has more than two decades of senior leadership experience in technology, safety, product, and incident response, including roles at Google, Uber, Meta, Reddit, and X.

#7120. Head of Research at CoinMarketCap

Our research already reaches millions each month through reports like our Monthly Exchange Report, Asset Watch and Market Pulse, and is cited by Bloomberg, WSJ, Forbes and Fortune. The next step is different. We are building CMC into a data platform for crypto, tokenized assets and broader markets, and research is how we prove the quality of that data to the people who buy it.

You will own our research agenda end to end: what we publish, how rigorous it is, and how it supports our enterprise data business. You will also be the senior voice on sensitive market analysis for CMC and its partners.

Set and run the research roadmap, from recurring flagships (monthly exchange report, weekly market and asset coverage) to deep dives on exchanges, tokenized assets, RWAs and market structure.

Build institutional grade research that showcases CMC data to banks, funds, data buyers and enterprise API clients, working closely with our API and BD teams on what buyers need.

Own methodology and data integrity: define how we measure volume, liquidity, flows and reserves, document it, and make sure every published number holds up to scrutiny.

Lead research on sensitive or fast moving market events, balancing speed, neutrality and accuracy, and handle review requests from partners with sound judgement.

Be a credible public face for CMC research with tier one media, on panels and in interviews.

Manage and develop a small research team, currently one research analyst, and use AI and automation to scale output without lowering quality.

Partner with marketing, editorial and product to turn research into formats people actually read and share, from long form reports to charts and short social posts.

A track record of published, data heavy research that institutions or serious media rely on.

Strong quantitative skills and hands on data work (SQL or Python, APIs, large datasets).

Excellent written English and the judgement to present sensitive findings neutrally and accurately.

#7117. AI Native Head Of Growth - EdTech (French fluent 🇫🇷) at Paradox

Most growth teams call themselves data-driven. They have a dashboard, an agency and a ChatGPT licence to write ad copy faster. That’s the 99%.

We want to be part of the 0.1%: a company where AI doesn’t just help the growth team, it runs the growth loop. Every day, on every channel.

Then it’s your chance to define a new category : The Operating System (OS) for Human Greatness Augmented with AI.

Science-backed. Design-led. AI-powered. Global.

We’re not looking for someone who only advises on what to test, we’re looking for someone who builds it. A hands-on Head of Growth who can ideate, write the copy, design the strategy and launch experiments directly.

1. Generate hypotheses. Read the signals (ad performance, webinar attendance, sales calls, objections, churn reasons, comments, competitors’ ads) and propose what to test next, with a cost and an expected impact.

2. Act across every tool. Not a chatbot in a tab: it reads and writes in our whole stack, from ad accounts to CRM, landing pages, webinars, email, social DMs and analytics.

3. Hold the full context of Paradox. Our DNA, offers, voice, member stories, past campaigns and what they taught us, in one shared company brain.

4. Ship its hypotheses. Produce the hook, the ad cut, the page, the email sequence or the funnel variant, and put it live.

5. Measure with real KPIs. Every test is tied to a number that matters (cost per qualified lead, show-up rate, close rate, CAC, payback, LTV), not to likes or reach.

6. Learn and loop on its own. Scale what works, kill what doesn’t, write the lesson back into the brain, and launch the next cycle without waiting for a meeting.

This is already real. Our growth hub tracks every hypothesis and experiment, with its result. An ads library of thousands of creatives feeds our creative research. Agents cut ads from long-form content, prepare webinars, wire Instagram comment-to-DM automations and audit our tracking after every live. Athena, our AI coach, works with members every day.

We believe self-help is broken: generic theory, a quick high, no real change.

We believe people deserve more. More than content. More than motivation.

They deserve a system that knows them, one that adapts, challenges, and grows with them.

Growth at Paradox means bringing people into something that actually changes their life, and proving it.

- Human Intelligence (coaches, peers, and mentors)

- Structured Intelligence (protocols, mental models, rituals)

- Technological Intelligence (AI personalization, feedback loops, adaptive journeys)

To create one seamless ecosystem: Paradox OS.

Paradox was founded by David Laroche, a creator followed by over 2 million people and known for his interviews with icons like Tony Parker, Mike Horn, Richard Branson and Gad Elmaleh. We started in French. We’re building for the world.

Here’s the bet: a company that learns by itself grows in a way a company of people alone can’t. A small growth team running a self-learning machine can out-test, out-learn and out-grow teams a hundred times its size.

We hold ourselves to the standards of Apple, Spotify and MasterClass. While others hide their numbers, we let our results speak.

As Head of Growth, you’ll be the architect of a growth machine that learns by itself. In a classic growth team, people do the work and AI helps. Here it’s the other way round: agents do the work, you design the system, set the bar and make the calls.

You don’t just manage a team, you get your hands dirty to identify growth levers and pull them yourself.

❌ The old way: a Head of Growth hires people and agencies, briefs them, and checks their work.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: your team is mostly agents, and you build them yourself. You write their skills, plug them into our tools (ad accounts, CRM, webinars, email, analytics) and write the evals that tell an agent what "good" means: a hook that stops the scroll, a page that converts, a test that’s worth its cost.

You give autonomy step by step: first an agent suggests, then it acts once you approve, then it acts alone within a budget and guardrails. You move each agent up a level only when its track record earns it.

Example: in your first month you build the agent that reviews every ad set overnight. At first it only suggests. By month three it pauses losers and moves budget by itself within limits you set, and you only see what was decided and why.

❌ The old way: a media buyer checks the dashboards each morning, changes bids by hand and waits for the weekly report.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: you build and improve the agents that watch every ad set, page, webinar and email sequence around the clock. They spot what’s dropping, find the cause, fix it or launch a variant, and report what they did.

You give autonomy step by step: an agent can pause an ad on its own long before it can raise a budget. You decide where each agent stops and a human takes over.

Example: show-up rate drops on Tuesday’s webinar. Before anyone notices, an agent has found the cause (a reminder that never went out), fixed it and started testing a new reminder sequence.

❌ The old way: a creative team briefs an agency, waits two weeks and gets five ads.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: you build the agents that turn every live, interview and member story into hooks, ad cuts and shorts, that study what the best advertisers in the world are running, and that write in our voice.

Your taste becomes the eval: you write down what makes a great hook, a great cut and a great story, in rules and examples the agents check themselves against. Each time you reject something, the reason goes back into the rules.

Example: one webinar becomes dozens of hook variants by the next morning. You keep the best few, and next week the agents’ first drafts are already closer to your bar.

❌ The old way: an analyst rebuilds the numbers in a spreadsheet at the end of the month, and nobody trusts them.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: you build the agents that connect every touch to cash, from ad to lead, live, sale and transformed member, and that audit the tracking after every campaign.

No number, no test: you define the KPIs that matter (CAC, show-up, close rate, LTV, payback), and agents refuse to launch a test that isn’t wired to one.

Example: after each live, an agent cross-checks the ad platform, the CRM and our analytics, finds where they disagree and fixes the leak before the next live.

❌ The old way: lessons live in people’s heads and leave with them.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: every result goes back into the company brain, and agents read it before proposing anything. The machine never learns the same lesson twice.

You measure the machine itself: tests per week, time from idea to live, the share of the work done without a human, and how many agents moved up an autonomy level this month. You push those numbers up.

And always: no fake scarcity, no fake claims, real stories told with consent. The machine can scale, but it doesn’t decide what’s right. You do.

How you’ll know you’re AI-native: you spend your time building agents, writing evals and making calls, not doing the work by hand. Most experiments are generated and shipped by agents. The output grows every month while the headcount doesn’t.

You’ll own these KPIs: LTV / CAC ratio, funnel conversion rate by stage, experiments launched per week.

Desired outcomes: conversion rates 2x better than baseline, growth experimentation that runs continuously, a predictable growth engine.

Tools you’ll master: GA4, Optibase, Vidalytics, Clarity, Hotjar, Amplitude, Figma, Looker Studio, VWO, Optimizely, Claude Code, and whatever helps you see what others miss.

You have ideally a minimum of 3 years of experience in Conversion Rate Optimization, Growth Experimentation, or Performance Marketing, ideally within SaaS, e-commerce, or digital product environments.

You have a track record of designing, implementing, and scaling A/B and multivariate tests that have generated measurable improvements in revenue, user activation, or conversion.

You’ve built with AI and agents yourself: Claude Code, automations, APIs, evals, creative pipelines. Show us something you built, not a certificate.

A Conversion Architect, skilled at diagnosing funnel bottlenecks and translating data into structured experimentation roadmaps that deliver measurable business outcomes.

A Behavioral Strategist, fascinated by what drives human action, able to mix cognitive psychology, UX, and persuasion frameworks to design experiences that convert because they resonate.

A Data-driven Creator, fluent in GA4, Hotjar, AB Tasty, VWO, Amplitude, not just running tests, but connecting insights to revenue, retention, and lifetime value.

Loop thinker: you see every channel as a loop that can learn, and you can’t stop yourself from closing it.

Taste: you know a great hook in two seconds, and you’d rather ship one great ad than ten average ones.

Numbers-honest: you say when a test failed. You care about real revenue, not vanity metrics.

Builder, not manager: you’d rather build the system than coordinate an agency.

Growth over ego: you love hard feedback, you read, study and learn fast.

Cross-disciplinary: you move from psychology to spreadsheet to creative brief to code.

Analytical: you turn a vague problem into a clear hypothesis, a metric and an experiment.

English: Fluent English, you can hold a conversation on specific details with English-speaking colleagues.

Our chosen language for written communication is English.

We work on compilers and toolchains, and we work on security, cryptography, performance and documentation. Some of our teams focus on packaging thousands of pieces of software, others focus on deep contributions to a single body of code.

If you are excited about the potential that open source has for humanity and the enterprise, you have outstanding results in your academic career, and you are excited to contribute to the open source stack, this is the best way to apply to Canonical. We will work with you to identify teams and projects that would be interesting and a good fit for your skills and motivations. The process is highly competitive, it will require effort and excellence to succeed if you apply here.

Most of our engineering teams work from home. We try to align a single team in a single time zone - EMEA (Europe, Middle East and Africa), APAC (Asia Pacific and Indian Ocean), and AMER (North, South and Central Americas). Even though you work from home, we try to ensure you always have colleagues alongside to coach and mentor you, and for the companionship of shared problem solving and pair programming. Larger projects have multiple teams and might span the globe. We work with the open source community and specialize in building teams that work well in the 'fishbowl' of open source engineering. This is full time work, five days a week, often solving hard problems with significant responsibility. We don't generally work weekends and nights and we do enjoy good holidays but we also expect productive and intense engagement Monday to Friday.

We bring all our engineering teams together in person twice a year for a deep 'sprint' with other teams to stimulate discussion and enable a wide range of career development paths. This is a fantastic way to demonstrate and develop your skill in software, learn about a wide range of technologies, make a meaningful impact to open source, and see some very interesting new cities that you might never otherwise get to visit.

If you find the open source stack amazing, and you have been a consistent high-performing student in your school and university, then this will be a challenging and exciting start to your career.

Location: This is a Globally remote role.

We consider geographical location, experience, and performance in shaping compensation worldwide. We assess performance and revisit compensation twice annually to ensure we recognize outstanding colleagues. In addition to base pay, we offer a performance-driven annual bonus or commission. We provide all team members with additional benefits which reflect our values and ideals. We balance our programs to meet local needs and ensure fairness globally.

Canonical is a pioneering tech firm at the forefront of the global move to open source. Our platform, Ubuntu, is very widely used in breakthrough enterprise initiatives such as public cloud, data science, AI, engineering innovation, and IoT. Our customers include the world's leading public cloud and silicon providers, and industry leaders in many sectors. We are founder-led, profitable, and growing.

Powered by worldwide distributed collaboration since our inception in 2004, we believe tech and business leadership can emerge anywhere. We have no central headquarters. Most of us work from home, but we are exceptionally disciplined about this. We have very high expectations of all colleagues, and are champions of excellence. Working here is a step into the future and will challenge you to think differently, work smarter, learn new skills, and raise your game.

We are proud to foster a workplace free from discrimination. Diversity of experience, perspectives, and background create a better work environment and better products. Whatever your identity, we will give your application fair consideration.

5. Measure with real KPIs. Retention, shares, saves, follower growth, and the followers who become leads and members. Not likes.

6. Learn and loop on its own. Double down on what works, kill what doesn’t, write the lesson back into the brain, and start the next cycle without waiting for a meeting.

This is already real. An ads library of thousands of creatives feeds our creative research. Agents cut ads from long-form content and wire Instagram comment-to-DM automations. And our product is AI-native too: Athena, our AI coach, works with members every day.

At Paradox, we are convinced that greatness is trainable, and we believe self-help is broken.

You lead the Content Factory not by management, but with creativity.

Take responsibility for the entire production chain from hooks to storyboarding the narrative, and ensuring the final cut matches the vision.

Own the packaging: titles, thumbnails and the first 3 seconds. You test variants every week and know your click-through and retention numbers by heart.

❌ The old way: one shoot becomes one video and a handful of posts.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: one shoot, one live or one interview becomes dozens of hooks, shorts, carousels and ad cuts by the next morning, in both languages. Concepts are tested with AI-generated drafts before we spend a day shooting them.

Your taste becomes the eval. You write down what makes a great hook, a great cut, a great carousel and a great story, in rules and examples the agents check themselves against. Each time you reject something, the reason goes back into the rules.

Act as the final quality check: nothing gets published if it doesn’t meet the "Paradox Standard" of aesthetics and storytelling, whether a person or an agent made it.

❌ The old way: a creative team briefs an editor, waits three days and gets two versions.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: agents draft, cut, caption, translate and schedule. You keep the best, and next week their first drafts are already closer to your bar.

Analyze retention graphs, CTR, and CPL to iterate based on feedback, killing what fails and aggressively scaling what works.

❌ The old way: someone pulls the stats at the end of the month and nobody changes anything.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: agents read the numbers of every post every day, explain why it worked or didn’t, and propose the next test. You measure the machine itself: posts per week, time from idea to live, hit rate, and the share of the work done without a human.

We started in French. You build the machine that takes Paradox to English-speaking audiences: channels, formats and voice that feel native, not translated.

And always: real stories told with consent, no fake claims, no fake scarcity. The machine can scale, but it doesn’t decide what’s right. You do.

Intolerant to Mediocrity: You have "Taste." You are physically pained by a bad font, a clumsy cut, or a weak headline. You fight for the details that others ignore, including in what AI produces.

Psychology Obsessed: You don’t just "make content"; you study human nature. You constantly ask why people click, why they share, and what emotion drives them.

A "Doer" with Authority: You command respect not by title, but by competence. You can jump into a project, write the script, direct the shoot, and fix the edit if needed.

Loop thinker: you see every platform as a loop that can learn, and you can’t stop yourself from closing it.

No Ego, Just Impact: You don’t fall in love with your ideas. You accept that even the best concepts can flop. When it happens, you don’t take it personally; you analyze the data, pivot immediately, and ship the next concept with renewed energy.

#7104. Anthropic: Data Center Controls Engineer

Headquarters: Remote-Friendly (Travel Required) | San Francisco, CA

About Anthropic

Anthropic’s mission is to create reliable, interpretable, and steerable AI systems. We want AI to be safe and beneficial for our users and for society as a whole. Our team is a quickly growing group of committed researchers, engineers, policy experts, and business leaders working together to build beneficial AI systems.

About the Role

Anthropic is seeking an experienced Datacenter Controls Engineer to join our Datacenter Infrastructure team. In this role, you will design, implement, and optimize control systems that support our global datacenter infrastructure, enabling the reliable operation of AI at scale.

You will work on cutting-edge control systems managing critical datacenter operations including liquid cooling systems (CDUs/CDMs), HVAC, electrical power monitoring systems, and building automation systems. This position requires both technical depth in industrial automation and the ability to collaborate across disciplines to deliver integrated solutions for high-density AI compute infrastructure.

Responsibilities

System Architecture & Integration

  • Design and implement multi-site controls architecture across Anthropic's global datacenter facilities

  • Define network topology, redundancy, and high-availability requirements for control nodes and servers

  • Develop and maintain standardized frameworks for BACnet, Modbus TCP/IP, OPC-UA, MQTT, and SNMP communication layers

  • Ensure integration between control systems and enterprise networks while maintaining security boundaries and compliance with cybersecurity frameworks for industrial control systems

Control System Design & Implementation

  • Define, design, develop, and implement control systems for datacenter mechanical, electrical, and building management systems with focus on AI compute environments

  • Develop SCADA/BMS applications using platforms including Ignition, Niagara/Tridium, or similar HMI/supervisory systems

  • Select and specify instrumentation including sensors, transmitters, actuators, and control valves for liquid cooling loops, power distribution, and environmental monitoring

Data Collection & Visualization

  • Architect and supervise deployment of centralized telemetry systems capable of storing high-resolution time-series data from power, thermal, and environmental sensors

  • Develop intuitive dashboards and visualization layers using Ignition SCADA, Grafana, or comparable frameworks for facilities, operations, and engineering teams

  • Leverage control systems to enable performance insights, fault detection, and predictive maintenance capabilities

Construction Integration & Documentation

  • Participate in new design and construction projects to ensure controls and BMS standards are embedded from design through commissioning

  • Define and review controls and instrumentation specifications in architectural and MEP packages

  • Create comprehensive technical documentation including P&IDs, control narratives, sequence of operations, and commissioning procedures

  • Validate installed systems during commissioning for proper network configuration, protocol compatibility, and data visibility

Minimum Qualifications

  • Bachelor's degree in Engineering (Electrical, Controls, Mechanical, Computer) or equivalent practical experience

  • 7+ years of experience in control systems design and implementation for industrial automation, building automation, or datacenter systems

  • Deep familiarity with industrial communication protocols: BACnet, Modbus TCP/IP, OPC-UA, MQTT, SNMP, Ethernet-IP

  • Experience with instrumentation selection, control panel design, and control system integration

  • Working knowledge of network architecture including subnets, VLANs, firewall rules, and IP management

  • Strong documentation skills and ability to work effectively in cross-functional teams

Preferred Qualifications

  • 10+ years in datacenter, critical facilities, or mission-critical infrastructure environments

  • Experience with liquid cooling systems including CDUs, CDMs, and cooling tower control systems

  • Background in high-density rack integration environments and AI/ML compute infrastructure

  • Experience designing or deploying BMS/SCADA systems such as Ignition, Niagara/Tridium, Wonderware, or similar

  • Strong background in database integration (SQL Server, InfluxDB, PostgreSQL, TimescaleDB) and data logging architecture

  • Proficiency in Python or scripting for automation/configuration within SCADA/BMS frameworks

  • Experience deploying multi-site redundant controls servers with high-availability requirements

  • Professional Engineering (PE) license or vendor certifications (Schneider, Rockwell, Siemens)

  • Knowledge of relevant codes and standards: ASHRAE, NFPA, IEEE, ISA

  • Familiarity with Grafana, Node-RED, Ignition Perspective, or equivalent visualization tools

  • Experience bridging cloud orchestration systems with facility-level power controls and load management (e.g., DCIM, BMS, EPMS)

The annual compensation range for this role is listed below. 

For sales roles, the range provided is the role’s On Target Earnings ("OTE") range, meaning that the range includes both the sales commissions/sales bonuses target and annual base salary for the role.

Annual Salary:
$320,000—$405,000 USD

Logistics

Minimum education: Bachelor’s degree or an equivalent combination of education, training, and/or experience

Required field of study: A field relevant to the role as demonstrated through coursework, training, or professional experience

Minimum years of experience: Years of experience required will correlate with the internal job level requirements for the position

Location-based hybrid policy: Currently, we expect all staff to be in one of our offices at least 25% of the time. However, some roles may require more time in our offices.

Visa sponsorship: We do sponsor visas! However, we aren't able to successfully sponsor visas for every role and every candidate. But if we make you an offer, we will make every reasonable effort to get you a visa, and we retain an immigration lawyer to help with this.

For roles performing work in California: We will consider for employment all qualified applicants, including those with arrest or conviction records or criminal histories, in a manner consistent with the requirements of applicable state and local laws, including the San Francisco Fair Chance Ordinance, the City of Los Angeles’ Fair Chance Initiative for Hiring Ordinance, the Los Angeles County Fair Chance Ordinance for Employers, and the California Fair Chance Act.

We encourage you to apply even if you do not believe you meet every single qualification. Not all strong candidates will meet every single qualification as listed.  Research shows that people who identify as being from underrepresented groups are more prone to experiencing imposter syndrome and doubting the strength of their candidacy, so we urge you not to exclude yourself prematurely and to submit an application if you're interested in this work. We think AI systems like the ones we're building have enormous social and ethical implications. We think this makes representation even more important, and we strive to include a range of diverse perspectives on our team.

Your safety matters to us. To protect yourself from potential scams, remember that Anthropic recruiters only contact you from @anthropic.com email addresses. In some cases, we may partner with vetted recruiting agencies who will identify themselves as working on behalf of Anthropic. Be cautious of emails from other domains. Legitimate Anthropic recruiters will never ask for money, fees, or banking information before your first day. If you're ever unsure about a communication, don't click any links—visit anthropic.com/careers directly for confirmed position openings.

How we're different

We believe that the highest-impact AI research will be big science. At Anthropic we work as a single cohesive team on just a few large-scale research efforts. And we value impact — advancing our long-term goals of steerable, trustworthy AI — rather than work on smaller and more specific puzzles. We view AI research as an empirical science, which has as much in common with physics and biology as with traditional efforts in computer science. We're an extremely collaborative group, and we host frequent research discussions to ensure that we are pursuing the highest-impact work at any given time. As such, we greatly value communication skills.

The easiest way to understand our research directions is to read our recent research. This research continues many of the directions our team worked on prior to Anthropic, including: GPT-3, Circuit-Based Interpretability, Multimodal Neurons, Scaling Laws, AI & Compute, Concrete Problems in AI Safety, and Learning from Human Preferences.

Come work with us!

Anthropic is a public benefit corporation headquartered in San Francisco. We offer competitive compensation and benefits, optional equity donation matching, generous vacation and parental leave, flexible working hours, and a lovely office space in which to collaborate with colleagues. Guidance on Candidates' AI Usage: Learn about our policy for using AI in our application process.

To apply: https://weworkremotely.com/remote-jobs/anthropic-data-center-controls-engineer
